The 4-fold Pandharipande–Thomas vertex and Jeffrey–Kirwan residue
Taro Kimura, Go Noshita

Abstract
We present a contour integral formalism for computing the K-theoretic equivariant Pandharipande–Thomas (PT) 4-vertex. Within the Jeffrey–Kirwan (JK) residue framework, we show that the PT 4-vertex can be obtained from the same integrand as the Donaldson–Thomas (DT) 4-vertex by choosing a different reference vector. We illustrate the formalism through examples involving curves and surfaces on the 4-fold. Furthermore, we investigate the DT/PT correspondence for the 4-fold setting together with its higher rank and supergroup-like generalizations.



1
Introduction

In the study of supersymmetric gauge theories and string theory, enumerative invariants of Calabi–Yau (CY) varieties play an essential role in counting certain stable bound states of D-branes, i.e., the BPS bound states. In the case of Calabi–Yau 3-folds, Donaldson–Thomas (DT) theory
Thomas:1998uj
and Pandharipande–Thomas (PT) theory
Pandharipande:2007kc
;
Pandharipande:2007sq
provide a mathematical way to count D6-D2-D0 bound states. Recently, there has been growing interest in extending these ideas to Calabi–Yau 4-folds
Cao:2014bca
;
Borisov:2017GT
;
Cao:2017swr
;
Cao:2019tnw
;
Cao:2019tvv
;
Oh:2020rnj
;
Monavari:2022rtf
;
Nekrasov:2017cih
;
Nekrasov:2018xsb
;
Nekrasov:2023nai
;
Bao:2024ygr
;
Szabo:2023ixw
, where new phenomena emerge due to the richer mathematical structure and physical perspective.

The
magnificent four

Nekrasov:2017cih
;
Nekrasov:2018xsb
;
Billo:2021xzh
is a physical setup whose partition function is given by the generating function of the enumerative invariants of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
. Such a setup was introduced along a series of studies
Nekrasov:2015wsu
;
Nekrasov:2016qym
;
Nekrasov:2016ydq
;
Nekrasov:2017rqy
;
Nekrasov:2017gzb
, where higher-dimensional generalizations and supersymmetric gauge theories arising from intersecting D-branes were introduced, i.e. the
gauge origami
framework
Nekrasov:2016gud
;
Nekrasov:2016qym
;
Rapcak:2018nsl
;
Rapcak:2020ueh
;
Pomoni:2021hkn
;
Pomoni:2023nlf
;
Fasola:2023ypx
;
Nekrasov:2017cih
;
Nekrasov:2018xsb
. In particular, in type IIA string theory, the magnificent four system arises from D8-branes wrapping the
\mathbb{C}^{4}
with D0-branes probing it. The flavored Witten index of the supersymmetric quantum mechanics of the D0-brane worldvolume theory then gives the partition function of the theory. Remarkably, this partition function is the generating function of solid partitions, the four-dimensional analogue of plane partitions, weighted by equivariant parameters corresponding to the
\Omega
-background on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

Extending this correspondence to general toric Calabi–Yau 4-folds requires a local building block analogous to the topological vertex
Maulik:2003rzb
;
Maulik:2004txy
;
Okounkov:2003sp
;
Iqbal:2007ii
;
Aganagic:2003db
;
Awata:2008ed
;
Nekrasov:2014nea
of the 3-fold case. In the DT4 theory, such a building block is called the equivariant DT4 vertex
Monavari:2022rtf
;
Nekrasov:2023nai
;
Piazzalunga:2023qik
;
DelZotto:2021gzy
, which encodes the contributions of solid partitions with prescribed asymptotics along the four complex coordinate axes of a local patch. The global enumerative invariants are then obtained by gluing these vertices according to the combinatorics of the toric diagram. Compared to the toric CY3 case, we can associate two types of asymptotics to the four directions. We can consider plane partitions as asymptotics of the four edges of the vertex or Young diagrams as asymptotics of the six faces of the vertex. Physically, the former correspond to considering D8-D2-D0 bound states, and the latter correspond to D8-D4-D0 bound states. One can combine both of them to consider the D8-D4-D2-D0 bound states.

In parallel, the equivariant PT4 vertex, based on stable pair theory for 4-folds, provides an alternative but equivalent description of the same geometry, differing in the underlying moduli problem while sharing the gluing formalism. The PT theory was first introduced to study the curve counting for 3-folds, showing the analytically better behavior and the numerical efficiency compared with the DT theory
Pandharipande:2007kc
;
Pandharipande:2007sq
. The PT theory was then formulated for 4-folds to study the curve counting as in the case of the 3-fold setups
Cao:2019fqq
;
Cao:2019tnw
;
Cao:2019tvv
;
Cao:2023lon
;
liu20234foldpandharipandethomasvertex
. Moreover, in particular to 4-fold setups, one may also consider the surface counting
Bae:2022pif
;
Bae:2024bpx
, which takes into account additional D4 branes for the D8-D2-D0 bound states in the context of string theory. Understanding the relationship between the DT and PT vertices is essential for a complete picture of curve counting and surface counting in complex dimension four.

The goal of this paper is to provide a method to compute both the equivariant DT4 and PT4 vertices within a systematical way using the Jeffrey–Kirwan (JK) residue formalism
Jeffrey1993LocalizationFN
;
Szenes2003ToricRA
;
Brion1999ArrangementOH
(see also
Benini:2013xpa
;
Benini:2013nda
;
Hori:2014tda
;
Hwang:2014uwa
;
Cordova:2014oxa
). Basically, this work is a 4-fold generalization of our previous work
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
. As discussed in
Nekrasov:2018xsb
, by studying the supersymmetric quantum mechanics of the D0-branes probing the D8-branes, the Witten index reduces to a finite-dimensional contour integral over the Cartan part of the corresponding gauge algebra. The JK-residue formalism organizes the poles of the integrand, which are naturally parametrized by solid partitions in this case. Moreover, the so-called
sign rules
, which is a characteristic naturally occurring in 4-folds, are automatically determined by construction. From this view point, the JK-residue formalism is a systematic way to perform explicit computations of the equivariant vertices, which automatically fixes all the normalizations in a natural way.

In this paper, we consider both the leg and surface boundary conditions, corresponding to the curve and the surface degrees of freedom in the 4-fold setups, where plane partitions and Young diagrams appear as asymptotics of the solid partition. From the viewpoint of supersymmetric quantum mechanics, the contribution coming from the framing node is modified depending on such an asymptotics. We will give a way to determine this framing node contribution and write down the contour integral. We then move on to how to evaluate the integral using the JK-residue formalism. The additional datum
\eta
, which we call the reference vector, determines whether we consider the DT vertex or the PT vertex. We will see that the standard reference vector
\eta=(1,\ldots,1)
gives the equivariant DT4 vertex, while the alternative reference vector
\eta=(-1,\ldots,-1)
gives the equivariant PT4 vertex. We perform explicit computations for the setups with both the leg and surface boundary conditions. A complete box-counting rule is yet to be clarified but our method gives an explicit way to study it. We also discuss the so-called DT/PT correspondence and have confirmed it for low levels. The existence of the sign rules so that DT/PT correspondence holds is an automatic consequence of our construction. Further generalizations, including the higher rank setup and the supergroup-like setup, of the DT/PT correspondence are also proposed.

The organization of this paper is as follows. We first review the magnificent four partition function and the JK-residue formalism in section
2
. We then discuss the DT4 counting with leg and surface boundary conditions using the JK-residue formalism in section
3
. We propose how to compute the equivariant PT4 vertex with leg boundary conditions using the JK-residue formalism in section
4
. We study several examples for the one-leg (section
4.1
), two-legs (section
4.2
), three-legs (section
4.3
), and the four-legs (section
4.4
). Similar computations for the equivariant PT4 vertex with surface boundary conditions are given in section
5
. Examples for the cases with one surface (section
5.1
), two surface (section
5.2
), and three surfaces (section
5.3
) are discussed. The DT/PT correspondence and the higher rank generalizations are discussed in section
6
. In Appendix
A
, we briefly review the JK-residue formalism for degenerate poles.

2
Magnificent four and JK-residue

Let us briefly review general formulas regarding the computation of the Witten index of the 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric quantum mechanics (SQM). See
Benini:2013xpa
;
Benini:2013nda
;
Hori:2014tda
;
Hwang:2014uwa
for details.

The 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM is obtained from a dimensional reduction of the 2d
\mathcal{N}=(0,2)
gauge theory. We have three basic multiplets: vector multiplet
V
, chiral multiplet
\Phi
, and the Fermi multiplet
\Lambda
. In this paper, we always consider unitary gauge groups. We also have the so-called
J
-term and
E
-terms. The
J
-term is a holomorphic function of the chiral fields coupled to the Fermi superfields. The
E
-term is also a holomorphic function of the chiral fields obeying
\overline{\mathcal{D}}\Lambda=E(\Phi)
, where
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
is the gauge covariant superderivative. We note that we also have the condition
\Tr(E(\Phi)\cdot J(\Phi))=0
for two supersymmetries.

Given the
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM, the Witten index is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\frac{1}{|W_{G}|}\oint_{\text{JK}}\mathcal{Z}_{V}\prod_{i}\mathcal{Z}_{\Phi_{i}}\prod_{\alpha}\mathcal{Z}_{\Lambda_{\alpha}}

(2.1)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{V}=\prod_{I}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{\alpha\in G}\operatorname{sh}(\alpha\cdot\phi),\quad\mathcal{Z}_{\Phi_{i}}=\prod_{\rho\in V_{\text{chiral}}}\frac{1}{\operatorname{sh}(\rho\cdot\mu)},\quad\mathcal{Z}_{\Lambda_{\alpha}}=\prod_{\rho\in V_{\text{Fermi}}}\operatorname{sh}(\rho\cdot\mu)

(2.2)

where the variable
\phi
parametrizes the Cartan subalgebra of the gauge group
G
, for which we abuse the notation of the corresponding root system, the
V_{\text{chiral}},V_{\text{Fermi}}
denote the representation of the chiral and Fermi superfields, by which we denote the corresponding sets of the weights as well, and
\mu
contains other parameters denoting the flavor fugacities of the theory.
We write

\displaystyle\operatorname{sh}(x)=2\sinh(x/2)=e^{x/2}-e^{-x/2}=[e^{x}].

(2.3)

For later use, we also introduce

\displaystyle\operatorname{ch}(x)=2\cosh(x/2)=e^{x/2}+e^{-x/2}.

(2.4)

Magnificent four

We denote the ten-dimensional space-time as
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}^{4}
. There are four complex one-planes and three-planes which we write as
\mathbb{C}_{a},\mathbb{C}^{3}_{\bar{a}}
for
a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}
and
\bar{a}\in\underline{\textbf{4}}^{\vee}
, where the four sets are defined as

\displaystyle\underline{\textbf{4}}=\{1,2,3,4\},\quad\underline{\textbf{4}}^{\vee}=\{123,124,134,234\}

(2.5)

and
\bar{S}
is the complement of
S
in
\underline{\textbf{4}}
. We have six complex two-planes
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{A}
for

\displaystyle A\in\underline{\textbf{6}}=\{12,13,14,23,34,34\}.

(2.6)

The magnificent four system is a low energy limit of
n
pairs of D8 and D8
′
branes wrapping the
\mathbb{C}^{4}
part.
1
1

1

In the original paper
Nekrasov:2017cih
, the D8
′
-brane is denoted as the
\overline{\text{D}8}
-brane. A physical realization of the magnificent four was also discussed in
Billo:2021xzh
by introducing D8-branes with some constant background field and D8
′
-branes without any background field. We rather follow the notation of
Billo:2021xzh
and call it the D8
′
-brane.
We can also introduce D0-branes to the system. The brane configuration is summarized as

\displaystyle\begin{tabular}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{C}_{1}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol &\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{C}_{2}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol &\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{C}_{3}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol &\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{C}_{4}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol &\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol \\
\cline{2-11}\cr&1&2&3&4&5&6&7&8&9&0\\
\hline\cr D0&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$\bullet$&$-$\\
\hline\cr$\text{D}8$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$\bullet$&$-$\\
\hline\cr$\text{D}8^{\prime}$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$-$&$\bullet$&$-$\\
\hline\cr\end{tabular}

(2.7)

Generally, we may also consider situations with different number of D8 and D8
′
branes
Billo:2021xzh
, but we always keep it to be the same in this paper.

Including suitable
B
-field
Witten:2000mf
, the low energy field theory of the
k
D0-branes is described by a 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM whose quiver diagram is described as

(2.8)

The black arrows are the chiral superfields
B_{a}\,(a\in\underline{\textbf{4}})
and the red arrows are the Fermi superfields
\Lambda_{i}
. The D8-D0 strings correspond to the chiral superfields
\mathsf{I}
, while the D8
′
-D0 strings correspond to the Fermi superfields
\Lambda_{\mathsf{I}}
. For the left quiver, we distinguished the flavor nodes for the D8 and D8’-branes. For later use, we shortly draw the quiver as the right figure, but note that the flavor fugacities of the D8 and D8’-branes are generally different.

The
E,J
-terms are

\displaystyle E_{i}=[\mathsf{B}_{4},\mathsf{B}_{i}]\quad J_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{ijk4}[\mathsf{B}_{j},\mathsf{B}_{k}]

(2.9)

for
i=1,2,3
, where
\varepsilon_{ijk4}
is the totally antisymmetric tensor with
\varepsilon_{1234}=1
. The system has a
U(1)^{3}
flavor symmetry coming from the rotation of the
\mathbb{C}_{a}
-plane:

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{a}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow q_{a}\mathsf{B}_{a},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathsf{I},\Lambda_{\mathsf{I}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathsf{I},\Lambda_{\mathsf{I}},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1,2,3}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow q_{4}q_{1,2,3}\Lambda_{1,2,3}

where
q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}=1
and
q_{a}=e^{\epsilon_{a}}
. For later use, we shortly write

\displaystyle q_{\mathcal{S}}=\prod_{a\in\mathcal{S}}q_{a},\quad\epsilon_{\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{S}}\epsilon_{a}

(2.11)

where
\mathcal{S}
is some set of indices.

The magnificent four partition function with
\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}8^{\prime}
-branes is then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\text{D}8}[\mathfrak{q},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{n};q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}[k]

(2.12)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\{\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha}\},\{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}\},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}),

(2.13)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\{\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha}\},\{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}\},\phi_{I})=\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I}),

(2.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})=\frac{1}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha})},\quad\mathcal{Z}^{{\text{D}8^{\prime}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})=\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J})=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\phi_{J})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{J}-\phi_{I})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{J}-\phi_{I}-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{J}-\phi_{I}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}

The parameters
\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}
denote the flavor fugacities of the framing node and we wrote
\mu_{\alpha}=e^{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}-\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha}}
.

Jeffrey–Kirwan (JK) residue

Given the contour integral formula of the Witten index, we need to evaluate the contour integral. The correct way to evaluate it is known to be the Jeffrey–Kirwan residue formalism
Jeffrey1993LocalizationFN
;
Szenes2003ToricRA
;
Brion1999ArrangementOH
(see als
Benini:2013xpa
;
Benini:2013nda
;
Hori:2014tda
;
Hwang:2014uwa
). We basically follow the Appendix of
Nawata:2023aoq
;
Kim:2024vci
.

Let the contour integral be

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\oint_{\text{JK}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{i}}{2\pi i}\mathcal{Z}(\phi)

(2.15)

where
\phi_{i}
takes values in the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of the gauge group. The poles come from the zeros of them in the denominator:

\displaystyle Q_{i}(\phi)+f_{i}(\mathfrak{a},\epsilon_{a})=0,\quad i=1,\ldots n

(2.16)

where
f_{i}
is some function depending on the parameters of the theory. Each pole corresponds to a hyperplane
H_{i}
in
\mathfrak{h}
and the charge vector
Q_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\ast}
is associated with it. The integrand is singular at
\mathcal{M}_{\text{sing}}=\cup_{i}H_{i}
and we denote
\mathcal{M}^{\ast}_{\text{sing}}
to be the set of isolated points where
n\geq k
linearly independent singular hyperplanes meet. When exactly
k
hyperplanes meet a point
\phi_{\ast}\in\mathcal{M}^{\ast}_{\text{sing}}
, the intersection is called
non-degenerate
, while when
n\geq k
hyperplanes meet, the intersection is called
degenerate
.

To perform the JK-residue formalism, we need an additional ingredient
\eta\in\mathfrak{h}^{\ast}
, which is called the reference vector. To explicitly show the reference vector dependence, we denote the contour integral as

\displaystyle\oint_{\eta}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{i}}{2\pi i}\mathcal{Z}(\phi).

(2.17)

For a generic reference vector, the contour integral is then evaluated as

\displaystyle\oint_{\eta}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{i}}{2\pi i}\mathcal{Z}(\phi)=\sum_{\phi_{\ast}\in\mathcal{M}_{\text{sing}}^{\ast}}\underset{\phi=\phi_{\ast}}{\text{JK-Res}}(Q(\phi_{\ast}),\eta)\mathcal{Z}(\phi).

(2.18)

If
\phi_{\ast}
is a non-degenerate pole associated with the charge vectors
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}
, the JK-residue is

\displaystyle\underset{\phi=\phi_{\ast}}{\text{JK-}\text{Res}}(Q(\phi_{\ast}),\eta)\mathcal{Z}(\phi)=\delta(Q,\eta)\frac{1}{|\det Q|}\underset{\delta_{k}=0}{\Res}\cdots\underset{\delta_{1}=0}{\Res}\left.\mathcal{Z}(\phi)\right|_{Q_{i}(\phi)+f_{i}(\mathfrak{a},\epsilon_{a})=\delta_{i}}

(2.19)

where

\displaystyle\delta(Q,\eta)=\begin{cases}1,\,\,\eta\in\text{Cone}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k})\\
0,\,\,\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\quad\text{Cone}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k})=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}Q_{i}=\eta\mid\lambda_{i}>0\right\}.

(2.20)

When the pole is a degenerate pole, we need more detailed analysis (see Appendix
A
).

In this paper, two choices of reference vectors will be used frequently:

\displaystyle\eta_{0}=(1,\ldots,1),\quad\tilde{\eta}_{0}=(-1,\ldots,-1).

(2.21)

Magnificent four and solid partitions

Let us use the JK-residue method to evaluate the contour integral of the rank one magnificent four partition function. The integrand is

\displaystyle\prod_{I=1}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J})

(2.22)

The denominators are

\displaystyle\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}),\quad\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})

(2.23)

and thus the poles are

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}=0,\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=0.

(2.24)

We denote the unit vector pointing the
i
-th direction in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
as
e_{i}
. The charge vectors are then associated to the poles as

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}=0

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad e_{I}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=0

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad e_{I}-e_{J}.

Let us choose the reference vector to be
\eta=\eta_{0}
and explicitly perform the JK-residue formalism. An interesting property is that the charge vectors need to form an oriented tree structure determined as follows.

•

We have
k
vertices labeled by
\{e_{I}\}_{I=1}^{k}
and arrows connecting them. Each vertex appears only once.

•

The root vertex correspond to the charge vectors
+e_{I}
.

•

The tree grows by adding a vertex
e_{K}
to a vertex
e_{J}
with an arrow
J\rightarrow K
. For each such arrow, the charge vector
e_{K}-e_{J}
.

A set of charge vectors chosen in this way obeys the condition that the reference vector lives in the cone
\eta\in\text{Cone}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k})
. See
Hwang:2014uwa
for a proof why this is true. We also note that if instead we choose the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, the charge vectors associated to the vertex
I
and the arrow
J\rightarrow K
will be
-e_{I}
and
e_{J}-e_{K}
. Namely, the signs will be flipped.

Although the above tree structure gives the poles to evaluate, not all of them have non-zero JK-residues. In particular, for the one instanton level, the possible choice of charge vector is
\{e_{1}\}
only and thus the pole picked up is
\phi_{1}-\mathfrak{a}=0
, which gives a non-zero JK-residue. For the second level, the possible choices are

\displaystyle\{e_{1},e_{2}\},

\displaystyle\phi_{1}-\mathfrak{a}=0,\,\,\phi_{2}-\mathfrak{a}=0,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\{e_{1},-e_{1}+e_{2}\},

\displaystyle\phi_{1}-\mathfrak{a}=0,\,\,\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}=\epsilon_{1,2,3,4},

\displaystyle\{e_{2},e_{1}-e_{2}\},

\displaystyle\phi_{2}-\mathfrak{a}=0,\,\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}=\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

The second and third comes from the Weyl invariance and eventually cancels with the Weyl group factor
k!
in the denominator. The first one actually cancels with the numerator
\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\phi_{J})^{2}
in the D0-D0 part. Omitting the degrees of freedom coming from the Weyl invariance, the poles giving non-zero JK-residues are

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{a}),\quad a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}.

(2.27)

Generally, the poles giving non-zero JK-residues are classified by a solid partition. A solid partition
\rho
is a sequence of non-integers
\{\rho_{a,b,c}\}
obeying the condition

\displaystyle\rho_{a,b,c}\geq\rho_{a+1,b,c},\quad\rho_{a,b,c}\geq\rho_{a,b+1,c},\quad\rho_{a,b,c}\geq\rho_{a,b,c+1}.

(2.28)

The size is defined as
|\rho|=\sum_{a,b,c}\rho_{a,b,c}
. The 4-cubes

in the solid partition are assigned coordinates in a natural way
(i,j,k,l)
and it obeys

\displaystyle(i,j,k,l)\in\rho\Leftrightarrow 1\leq l\leq\rho_{i,j,k}.

(2.29)

Boxes in the solid partition obeys the melting rule.

Conditions 2.1
.

If any of

\displaystyle(i+1,j,k,l),\quad(i,j+1,k,l),\quad(i,j,k+1,l),\quad(i,j,k,l+1)

(2.30)

are contained in the box configurations, then
(i,j,k,l)
is also included. In other words, the boxes are stacked in a way as if the gravity is pointing the
(-1,-1,-1,-1)
direction.

The poles are classified by the boxes of the solid partition.

Theorem 2.1

(
Nekrasov:2017cih
;
Nekrasov:2018xsb
)
.

The poles of the rank one magnificent four giving non-zero JK-residue are classified as

\displaystyle\{\phi_{I}\}_{I=1}^{k}\longrightarrow\{c_{\underline{\textbf{4}},\mathfrak{a}}(\scalebox{0.04}{\hbox to199.49pt{\vbox to219.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.61938pt\lower-61.61938pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

})=\mathfrak{a}+(i-1)\epsilon_{1}+(j-1)\epsilon_{2}+(k-1)\epsilon_{3}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}.

})=vq_{1}^{i-1}q_{2}^{j-1}q_{3}^{k-1}q_{4}^{l-1},\quad v=e^{\mathfrak{a}}.

(2.33)

Tuning the parameter
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{a}
, the framing node contribution reduces to the D6-D0 configuration:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{a}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{a})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})}.

(2.34)

Note that we also have the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})=\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{a}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{a},\phi_{I}).

(2.35)

Generally setting
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{a}+\sum_{a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}}n_{\bar{a}}\epsilon_{a}
, we obtain the tetrahedron instanton partition function with
n_{\bar{a}}
D6
{}_{\bar{a}}
-branes.

Plethystic exponential (PE) formula

The magnificent four partition function actually has a plethysic exponential (PE) formula
2
2

2

We also note that after introducing proper Chern-Simons terms, one can also obtain a PE formula for the case when we do not have the D8’-branes
Noshita:2025bzg
.

Nekrasov:2017cih
;
Nekrasov:2018xsb
;
Kool:2025qou
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}_{n}[\mathfrak{q},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{n};q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{PE}\left[\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{\left[\prod\limits_{\alpha}\mu_{\alpha}\right]}{\left[\mathfrak{q}\prod\limits_{\alpha}\mu_{\alpha}^{-1/2}\right]\left[\mathfrak{q}\prod\limits_{\alpha}\mu_{\alpha}^{1/2}\right]}\right]

(2.36)

where the PE formula is defined as

\displaystyle\text{PE}[f(x_{1},\ldots x_{n})]=\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell}f(x_{1}^{\ell},\ldots,x_{n}^{\ell})\right).

(2.37)

For the rank one case, we simply have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{PE}\left[\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{\left[\mu\right]}{\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{-1/2}\right]\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{1/2}\right]}\right].

(2.38)

For later use, we introduce

\displaystyle\text{MF}[\mu]=\text{PE}\left[\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{\left[\mu\right]}{\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{-1/2}\right]\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{1/2}\right]}\right]

(2.39)

and the rank
n
magnificent four partition function is obtained by
\mu=\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mu_{\alpha}
.

An interesting property is that we have the following reflection formula

\displaystyle\text{MF}[\mu^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\text{PE}\left[\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{\left[\mu^{-1}\right]}{\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{+1/2}\right]\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{-1/2}\right]}\right]

(2.40)

\displaystyle=\text{PE}\left[-\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{\left[\mu\right]}{\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{+1/2}\right]\left[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{-1/2}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{MF}[\mu]^{-1}

where we used
[\mu^{-1}]=-[\mu]
.

3
DT4 counting and JK-residue

In this section, we review the JK-residue formalism of the equivariant Donaldson–Thomas (DT) 4-vertex. While the magnificent partition function is understood as a character of the infinite dimensional module parametrized by the solid partitions with nontrivial weights depending on the equivariant parameters
q_{1,2,3,4}
, the DT4 vertex is a weighted character of solid partitions with nontrivial boundary conditions. In particular, we will discuss two-types of boundary conditions, the leg boundary conditions and the surface boundary conditions. We also discuss what will happen for the case with both types of the boundary conditions. Physically, we are considering the partition function of the D8-D4-D2-D0 bound states.

3.1
DT4 counting with boundary conditions

Leg boundary conditions

Let us start from the leg boundary condition case. A solid partition with leg boundary conditions is a configuration with asymptotic
finite
plane partitions in the four legs of the solid partition. We denote the four plane partitions as
\pi_{1,2,3,4}
. The plane partition is described as a two-dimensional array of non-negative integers such as
\pi_{a}=\{\pi_{a,ij}\}
obeying the conditions

\displaystyle\pi_{a,ij}\geq\pi_{a,i+1,j},\quad\pi_{a,ij}\geq\pi_{a,i,j+1}.

(3.1)

The orientation of the partition is given in a symmetric way. Namely, for the plane partition
\pi_{a}
,
\pi_{a,ij}
is the number of boxes stacked in the
a+3
-direction and the two-dimensional base is the
(a+1,a+2)
-plane, where
a
is understood modulo four.

To illustrate it, we decompose the solid partition into sequences of plane partitions in the fourth-direction.
3
3

3

This is the
(1,3)
-decomposition in
Nekrasov:2017cih
Under this decomposition, we obtain a non-increasing sequence of plane partitions with boundary conditions. The asymptotic plane partitions
\pi_{1,2,3}
will be described as non-increasing sequence of Young diagrams, while the plane partition
\pi_{4}
is a constant sequence of plane partition. The minimal solid partition with no additional boxes except the asymptotic plane partitions looks like

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/minimalsolid_leg.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(3.2)

where the plane partitions
\pi_{1,2,3}
are colored in light blue, and the plane partition
\pi_{4}
is colored in orange. We first placed the plane partitions
\pi_{1,2,3}
and the boxes of
\pi_{4}
not included in
\pi_{1,2,3}
are drawn in orange. Note that
\pi_{4}
is extending semi-infinitely in the positive direction of the 4-axis.

For later use, we also identify the plane partitions
\pi_{1,2,3,4}
with the corresponding character given by the box contents defined as

\displaystyle\pi_{a}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\pi_{a,ij}}q_{a+1}^{i-1}q_{a+2}^{j-1}q_{a+3}^{k-1}.

(3.3)

For example, the plane partition
\pi_{3}
is

\displaystyle\pi_{3}=(1+q_{1}+q_{2})+q_{4}(1+q_{1})+q_{4}^{2}.

(3.4)

Surface boundary conditions

A solid partition with surface boundary conditions is a configuration with asymptotic
finite
Young diagrams in the six surfaces of the solid partition. We denote the six Young diagrams as
\lambda_{A}\,(A\in\underline{\textbf{6}})
depending on which plane the Young diagram extends semi-infinitely. One may also impose an orientation on such Young diagrams but we omit the discussion.

Similar to the leg boundary conditions case, we illustrate such solid partitions by decomposing it in the fourth direction. This time, the boundary Young diagrams
\lambda_{14,24,34}
are visualized as sequence of Young diagrams constant in the fourth-direction:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/minimalsolid_surface1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(3.5)

On the other hand, the Young diagrams
\lambda_{12,23,31}
are visualized as non-increasing 1d partitions extending in the
3,1,2
directions respectively:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/minimalsolid_surface2.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(3.6)

where we colored the surfaces in the 123-plane in light green.

For generic boundary conditions
\{\lambda_{A}\}_{A\in\underline{\textbf{6}}}
, the minimal solid partition looks like

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/minimalsolid_surface3.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(3.7)

Note that the set of the light blue boxes are also extending semi-infinitely in the fourth-direction.

The Young diagrams
\lambda_{A}
are identified with the character with the box contents

\displaystyle\lambda_{ab}=\sum_{(i,j)\in\lambda_{ab}}q_{a}^{i-1}q_{b}^{j-1}.

(3.8)

For example, for
\lambda_{24}
in (
3.7
), we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{24}=1+q_{2}+q_{3}.

(3.9)

3.2
Framing node and JK-residue

In this section, we derive the contour integral formulas whose poles are classified by solid partitions with the leg and surface boundary conditions. As mentioned in section
2
, the information of the contour integrand and the reference vector controls how the integral is evaluated. In this section, we keep the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
and construct the framing node contribution producing solid partitions with boundary conditions.

To construct the framing node contribution, one can start from analyzing the D8-D2-D0 or D8-D4-D0 bound states and construct a 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM. Additional superfields and
J,E
-terms will be added to the system and the Witten index of it gives the contour integral. See for example
Galakhov:2021xum
and
(
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
, section 3)
for a discussion of this direction for the D6-D2-D0 case. We will not take this direction in this paper, because a detailed classification and derivation of the
J,E
-terms is essential. Instead, we will use the infinite product construction discussed in
(
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
, section 3.2)
.

Leg boundary conditions

Let us start from the leg boundary conditions. We first introduce the following set of boxes

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{a,\pi_{a}}=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\mid x_{a}=1,\ldots,\infty,(x_{b},x_{c},x_{d})\in\pi_{a},\,\,(b,c,d\neq a)\}

(3.10)

for
a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}
. They are boxes living at the four legs of the solid partition. We then define

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}\coloneqq\sum_{a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}}\mathcal{B}_{a,\pi_{a}}-\sum_{ab\in\underline{\textbf{6}}}\mathcal{B}_{ab,\pi_{a}\cap\pi_{b}}+\sum_{\overline{(abc)}\in\underline{\textbf{4}}}\mathcal{B}_{abc,\pi_{a}\cap\pi_{b}\cap\pi_{c}}-\mathcal{B}_{\underline{\textbf{4}},\pi_{1}\cap\pi_{2}\cap\pi_{3}\cap\pi_{4}}

(3.11)

where
\mathcal{B}_{ab,\pi_{a}\cap\pi_{b}}=\mathcal{B}_{a,\pi_{a}}\cap\mathcal{B}_{b,\pi_{b}},\,\,\mathcal{B}_{abc,\pi_{a}\cap\pi_{b}\cap\pi_{c}}=\bigcap\limits_{i=a,b,c}\mathcal{B}_{i,\pi_{a}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\underline{\textbf{4}},\cap_{a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}}\pi_{a}}=\bigcap\limits_{a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}}\mathcal{B}_{a,\pi_{a}}
. The boxes in
\mathcal{B}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}
are the boxes of the minimal solid partition with the leg boundary conditions.

Recalling the JK-residue prescription, whenever a box is added at
c_{\underline{\textbf{4}},\mathfrak{a}}(\scalebox{0.03}{\hbox to199.49pt{\vbox to219.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.61938pt\lower-61.61938pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

appears and modifies the contour integrand. To include boundary conditions, we need to include the boundary boxes
\mathcal{B}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}
and thus a natural candidate is

}),\phi_{I}).

(3.12)

However, since the number of boxes in
\mathcal{B}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}
is infinite, we need to regularize the infinite product appearing here.

To make the discussion concrete, let us consider the case
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4})=(\varnothing,\varnothing,\varnothing,\hbox to7.13pt{\vbox to7.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 6.72 L 6.72 6.72 L 6.72 0 Z M 6.72 6.72 M 0 6.72 L 2.59 9.31 L 9.31 9.31 L 6.72 6.72 M 6.72 0 L 9.31 2.59 L 9.31 9.31}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
. The boundary boxes at
\mathcal{B}_{4,\pi_{4}}
give

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}(l-1),\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{41,42,43})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2,3})}

(3.13)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{4}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I}).

We also have the relation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{4}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})}{\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{4}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4},\phi_{I})}=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I}).

(3.14)

Using the quadrality, we also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{a}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{a})}\prod_{i\in\bar{a}}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{i})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{i})}.

(3.15)

Combinatorially, these contributions correspond to a sequence of boxes extending semi-infinitely in the positive direction of the
a
-axis starting from
\mathfrak{a}
. We call such one-dimensional objects as
rods
and specify the direction it extends as
a
-rod
(a\in\underline{\textbf{4}})
. To obtain the regularized formula of general boundary conditions, one first decompose
\mathcal{B}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}
into non-intersecting one-dimensional rods and then associated to each of them the D2-D0 factors. For example, when we have at most one box at each leg, the framing node contributions are given as follows:

Surface boundary conditions

For the surface boundary conditions, we denote the set of boxes living at the six surfaces as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{A,\lambda_{A}}=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\mid x_{a,b}=1,\ldots,\infty\,\,(a,b)\in A,\quad(x_{c},x_{d})\in\lambda_{A}\,(c,d\in\bar{A})\}

(3.20)

and the union of it as
\mathcal{B}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}_{A\in\underline{\textbf{6}}}}
. The framing node contribution is

{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 83.55 -4.81 L 152.67 -73.93}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 12.77 27.29 L -46.62 -19.42}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 102.6 27.87 L 178.5 -20}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -4.81 83.55 L -73.93 152.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 83.55 83.55 L 152.67 152.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 14.27 115.85 L -48.13 206.99}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 101.27 115.41 L 179.84 207.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}),\phi_{I}).

(3.21)

Again, since the product is an infinite product, we need to regularize it properly.

Let us consider the case when we have one surface
\lambda_{12}=1
. The boundary contributions are

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(i-1)\epsilon_{1}+(j-1)\epsilon_{2},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{34})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4})}

(3.22)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I}).

Note that we also have the relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{2}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(i-1)\epsilon_{1},\phi_{I}),

(3.23)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})}{\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2},\phi_{I})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{2}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I}),

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})=\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I}).

(3.24)

Using the quadrality symmetry, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{A}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{\bar{A}})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{c})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{d})},\quad c,d\in\overline{A}.

(3.25)

Combinatorially, such contribution corresponds to adding a two-dimensional surface in the
A
-plane starting from the coordinate
\mathfrak{a}
.

For generic boundary conditions, we need to decompose the minimal solid partition into non-intersecting surfaces and use this formula. However, generally, the minimal solid partition is not decomposed only in the surfaces but one-dimensional rods are also necessary. For example, for the case with
\lambda_{12}=\lambda_{13}=1
, the minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/surface_ex1.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(3.26)

We can decompose it into a 12-surface with the coordinate
\mathfrak{a}
, a 34-surface with the coordinate
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}
, and a one-dimensional 4-rod with the coordinate
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}
. Thus, the framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})

(3.27)

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{34}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{4}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4},\phi_{I}).

Choice of contour

Although the above derivations determine the contour integrand, to evaluate the JK-residue, we need to specify which poles are picked up by the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
. Such choice is related with whether the corresponding chiral multiplets are fundamental or anti-fundamental with respect to the D8-branes. To understand this property, one needs to study the 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM in detail. In this paper, instead we practically determine them in the following way. An observation is that the poles of the contour integrand can be classified into poles corresponding to the coordinates of the addable boxes of the minimal solid partitions and poles that do not. For poles that do not correspond to the addable boxes, we flip them using

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-c_{\underline{\textbf{4}},\mathfrak{a}}(\scalebox{0.03}{\hbox to199.49pt{\vbox to219.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.61938pt\lower-61.61938pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 83.55 83.55 L 152.67 152.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 14.27 115.85 L -48.13 206.99}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 101.27 115.41 L 179.84 207.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}))}.

(3.28)

Note that the numerator part does not affect how one performs the contour integral.

For example, for the one leg one box case, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\varnothing\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 4.48 L 4.48 4.48 L 4.48 0 Z M 4.48 4.48 M 0 4.48 L 1.73 6.21 L 6.21 6.21 L 4.48 4.48 M 4.48 0 L 6.21 1.73 L 6.21 6.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{41,42,43})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2,3})}

(3.29)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{41,42,43})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2,3})}

where we flipped the sign in the second line. In the first line the poles
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1,2,3}
correspond to the addable boxes while the others do not and so we flip
\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4})
.

For the one surface case, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{34})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4})}

(3.30)

and all of the poles correspond to the addable boxes and thus we do not need to flip any term of the denominator.

In this paper, the framing node contribution is always implicitly written in this way. We then define the DT4 partition functions using this framing node contribution.

Definition 3.1
.

The DT4 partition function or the equivariant DT4 vertex with leg boundary conditions is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}[k],

(3.31)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}[k]=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\eta_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\mathcal{Z}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(3.32)

The pole structure of the framing node contribution is obtained as discussed above.

Definition 3.2
.

The DT4 partition function with surface boundary conditions is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[k],

(3.33)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[k]=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\eta_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\mathcal{Z}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(3.34)

Again, the pole structure of the framing node contribution is obtained as discussed above.

Taking the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
, one can show that the poles are indeed classified by solid partitions with the boundary conditions.

4
PT4 counting with leg boundary conditions

In this section, we explicitly perform the Pandharipande-Thomas (PT) counting for four-folds with leg boundary conditions for various situations. For the moment, a complete combinatorial rule for the PT4 counting seems to be not available yet especially for the four-legs case. See
Cao:2019tnw
;
Cao:2019tvv
;
liu20234foldpandharipandethomasvertex
for reference.

In this paper, we follow the strategy given in
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
and propose that PT4 counting is obtained by changing the reference vector from
\eta_{0}
to
\tilde{\eta}_{0}
. The main claim of this section is the following proposal.

Claim 4.1
.

The PT4 partition function or the equivariant PT4 vertex with leg boundary conditions is computed by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}[k],

(4.1)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}[k]=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\mathcal{Z}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(4.2)

The JK-residue formalism provides which poles to evaluate the residues and the non-zero JK-residue poles have a one-to-one correspondence with the PT4 box configurations (see for a similar analysis for the PT3 case in
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
). For the moment, we do not have a complete classification of the poles and box-counting rules and we leave a complete description of them for future work.

We also note that when considering partition functions regarding D8-branes, one needs to deal with the so-called
sign rules
. Such sign rule is a consequence of how one takes the JK-residue and compare it with index computations
Nekrasov:2018xsb
. In this sense, the JK-residue formalism automatically fixes them in a natural way.

Before moving on to explicit computations, let us briefly discuss how we illustrate PT4 configurations. Following the description to derive PT3 configurations, we first start from a minimal solid partition

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4generalrules_1.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(4.3)

Note again that we are decomposing the solid partitions into slices of plane partitions.

We then extend the four legs in the negative directions of each axis and then remove the boxes living in the positive quadrant, which forms a hollow structure:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4generalrules_2.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\cdots\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4generalrules_3.pdf}}}}}}}$}

The first line has positive coordinates
x_{4}>0
of the 4-axis and the second line has non-positive coordinates
x_{4}\leq 0
of the 4-axis. Note that the second line extends semi-infinitely to the left. The
blue
boxes live at the intersection of exactly two legs, the
red
boxes live at the intersection of exactly three-legs, and the
black
boxes live at the intersection of the four-legs. Obviously, due to the existence of the four-legs, each three-dimensional slice do not look like a PT3 configuration anymore.

The boxes of the PT4 configurations will live inside the hollow structure. For the one-leg and two-legs cases, the boxes obey the following melting rule.

Conditions 4.1
.

If any of

\displaystyle(i-1,j,k,l),\quad(i,j-1,k,l),\quad(i,j,k-1,l),\quad(i,j,k,l-1)

(4.5)

are contained in the box configurations, then
(i,j,k,l)
is also included. In other words, the boxes are stacked in a way as if the gravity is pointing the
(1,1,1,1)
direction.

For the three-legs and four-legs cases, additional rules for the red boxes and black boxes will be necessary
Pandharipande:2007sq
;
Pandharipande:2007kc
;
liu20234foldpandharipandethomasvertex
, but we will not discuss them in this paper.

In the JK-residue computations, we will see that for the one-leg and two-legs cases, the contour integrands have only first order poles and the poles correspond to the boxes obeying Cond.
4.1
above. Namely, the PT4 counting rule is similar to the PT3 case. For the three-legs case, during the JK-residue procedure, second-order poles appear similar to the PT3 counting. On the other hand, for the four-legs case, the situation is much more complicated and during the JK-residue procedure, we will encounter third-order poles. This is the reason we need additional rules other than the previous condition. Such subtleties will be discussed elsewhere.

4.1
One-leg

One D2
3
-brane

Let us first study a well-known example, where the leg boundary conditions are

\displaystyle\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=\pi_{4}=\varnothing,\quad\pi_{3}=1.

(4.6)

The minimal solid partition is illustrated as

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/minimalsolid_oneleg_1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(4.7)

The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 4.48 L 4.48 4.48 L 4.48 0 Z M 4.48 4.48 M 0 4.48 L 1.73 6.21 L 6.21 6.21 L 4.48 4.48 M 4.48 0 L 6.21 1.73 L 6.21 6.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\varnothing}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{3}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{31,32,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2,4})}

and the contour integrand is

\displaystyle\prod_{I=1}^{k}\mathcal{Z}_{\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 4.48 L 4.48 4.48 L 4.48 0 Z M 4.48 4.48 M 0 4.48 L 1.73 6.21 L 6.21 6.21 L 4.48 4.48 M 4.48 0 L 6.21 1.73 L 6.21 6.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\varnothing}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(4.9)

If we choose the reference vector to be
\eta=\eta_{0}=(1,\ldots,1)
, the poles picked up at the level one is

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1,2,4}

(4.10)

which correspond to the boxes that we can add to the minimal solid partition. Such choice of reference vector gives the DT4 counting.

Instead, let us choose the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}=(-1,\ldots,-1)
, the poles come from

\displaystyle-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}=0,\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}+\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=0.

(4.11)

Using the Weyl-invariance, we can assume that the poles are picked up in the order
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{k}
and

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3},\quad\phi_{I}=\phi_{J}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}\quad(I>J).

(4.12)

For level one, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}.

(4.13)

For level two, the candidate of the poles are
\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3},\,\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}
. The pole
\phi_{2}=fra-\epsilon_{3}=\phi_{1}
is canceled by the numerator
\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\phi_{J})
of
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J})
. The poles

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,4}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{31,32,34}

(4.14)

are canceled by the numerator of
\mathcal{Z}_{\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 4.48 L 4.48 4.48 L 4.48 0 Z M 4.48 4.48 M 0 4.48 L 1.73 6.21 L 6.21 6.21 L 4.48 4.48 M 4.48 0 L 6.21 1.73 L 6.21 6.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\varnothing}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})
. Thus, the pole coming from
\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{3}
only remains.

Recursively, we can see that the poles

\displaystyle\phi_{I}=\mathfrak{a}-I\epsilon_{3},\quad I=1,\ldots,k

(4.15)

only remain. The PT4 configuration is understood as stacking boxes in the hollow structure extending in the negative direction of the 3-direction:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_oneleg_1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(4.16)

The JK-residue is then computed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\underset{\phi_{k}=\mathfrak{a}-k\epsilon_{3}}{\Res}\cdots\underset{\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}}{\Res}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\mathcal{Z}_{\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\varnothing}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J})

(4.17)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a}+i\epsilon_{3})}{\operatorname{sh}(i\epsilon_{3})}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{[\mu q_{3}^{i}]}{[q_{3}^{i}]}

where
\mu=e^{b-\mathfrak{a}}
. The PT4 partition function is then
(
Monavari:2022umi
, Lemma 6.5.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

(4.18)

Note that setting
\mu=q_{4}
, we simply obtain the PT3 vertex with one-leg and one-box.

Multiple D2
3
-branes

As a nontrivial example, let us consider the case with

\displaystyle\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=\pi_{4}=\varnothing,\quad\pi_{3}=1+q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{4}.

(4.19)

Starting from the minimal solid partition, extending it in the negative direction and removing the boxes in the positive quadrant gives

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_oneleg_2.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(4.20)

The boxes are then stacked inside this hollow structure in a way such that the gravity is pointing the
(1,1,1,1)
direction. Let us list down the configurations following this melting rule up to the second level. For level one, we can place boxes at
-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1,2,4}
, where we identified the coordinates with the
\epsilon
-coordinates and omit the origin
\mathfrak{a}
for simplicity. For level two, we have the following configurations

\displaystyle(-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{4}-2\epsilon_{3}),

\displaystyle(-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{3}),\quad(\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3})

(4.21)

\displaystyle(-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1},-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4}),

\displaystyle(-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{2},-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4}),\quad(-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1},-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{2}).

Each slice of the PT4 configuration is a PT3 configuration and due to the gravity also pointing the
(0,0,0,1)
direction, boxes need to be supported from the positive
4
-direction.

Let us check this using the JK-residue formalism. First, the framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\pi_{4}}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(b-\phi_{I})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{2})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{4})}{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{3}-\phi_{I})\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}-\phi_{I})\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{3}-\phi_{I})}

(4.22)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{124})^{2}}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{1,2})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2}-\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{4})}.

Choosing the reference vector to be
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, at the first level, the poles picked up are

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1,2,4}

(4.23)

which indeed matches with the configuration discussed above. For the next level, the poles picked up are

\displaystyle\phi_{I}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1,2,4},\quad\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(4.24)

We have two classes of poles. The first class of poles comes from picking two out of the three
\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1,2,4}
. All of them do not vanish and we have

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=(\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1},\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{2}),\quad(\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{2},\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4}),\quad(\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1},\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4})

(4.25)

up to Weyl invariance.

The other class of poles comes from
\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}
which extends the growth of the crystal. Due to the triality between
1,2,4
, let us focus on the case with
\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1}
. The next pole comes from
\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{23,34},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{3}
. The pole
\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}
vanishes from the
\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{124})^{2}
coming from the numerator of the framing node contribution. The poles
\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{23,34}=\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{4,2}
are canceled by the numerators

\displaystyle\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}),\quad\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{4}).

(4.26)

Thus, only
\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{3}
gives non-zero JK-residues. Using the triality symmetry the following contributions remain

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1,2,4}-\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1,2,4}-2\epsilon_{3}).

(4.27)

Therefore, we indeed obtain the configurations in (
4.21
).

Multiple D2
4
-branes

Since we are using the
(1,3)
-decomposition, the PT4 configuration looks different when the leg is extending in the 4-direction. For the example (
4.19
), considering the minimal solid partition, extending it in the negative direction and removing the boxes in the positive quadrant, we obtain

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_oneleg_3.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(4.28)

Note that the rightmost slice has the coordinate
-\epsilon_{4}
and the slices extends semi-infinitely to the left. The PT4 configurations are stack of boxes inside this hollow structure. In this description, each slice of the PT4 configuration looks like a reversed plane partition, and boxes are stacked from the right to the left in the 4-direction.

4.2
Two-legs

Let us then move on to the two-legs case. For this case, the situation is similar to the one-leg case and the PT4 box-counting rule is simple. The resulting PT4 configurations obey only the melting rule with the gravity pointing the
(1,1,1,1)
direction.

Let us first study the case with the boundary conditions

\displaystyle\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=1,\quad\pi_{3}=\pi_{4}=\varnothing.

(4.29)

See the computation for the three-fold version in
(
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
, secton 3.4.2)
.

The hollow structure is given as

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_twoleg_1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(4.30)

The PT configurations are similar to the three-fold case. For level one, we have a box at the origin. For generic levels, we have multiple boxes stacked in the negative directions of the 1,2-axes.

Let us derive these PT configurations using the JK-residue formalism. The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{1}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{1}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2},\phi_{I})

(4.31)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{b})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{12})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3,4})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3,4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{12})}.

Choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, for level one, the pole picked up is

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}.

(4.32)

The JK-residue is evaluated as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 4.48 L 4.48 4.48 L 4.48 0 Z M 4.48 4.48 M 0 4.48 L 1.73 6.21 L 6.21 6.21 L 4.48 4.48 M 4.48 0 L 6.21 1.73 L 6.21 6.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\varnothing\varnothing}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{1})=-\frac{[\mu][q_{12}][q_{13}][q_{23}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}.

(4.33)

For the second level, the poles come from
\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}
. The poles
\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{3,4}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3,4}
are canceled by the numerators
\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3,4})
from the numerators of the framing node contribution. The non-zero contributions come from

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2}.

(4.34)

Recursively, at the generic level, one can show that the configurations giving non-zero JK-residues are classified by two non-negative integers
m,n\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\{\phi_{I}\}_{I=1}^{k}=\{\mathfrak{a},\,\,\mathfrak{a}-i\epsilon_{1},\,\,\mathfrak{a}-j\epsilon_{2}\mid i=1,\ldots,m,\,j=1,\ldots,n\},

(4.35)

where
m,n
are the number of boxes extending in the 1,2-directions respectively. Denoting such configuration as
[m,n]
, the JK-residue is evaluated as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m+n+1}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{m+n+1}\underset{\phi=\phi_{[m,n]}}{\Res}\prod_{I}\mathcal{Z}_{\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\varnothing\varnothing}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J})

(4.36)

\displaystyle=\frac{[\mu][q_{34}]}{[q_{4}][q_{3}]}\times\frac{[q_{3}q_{1}^{m+1}q_{2}^{-n}][q_{4}q_{1}^{m+1}q_{2}^{-n}]}{[q_{1}^{m+1}q_{2}^{-n}][q_{1}^{m}q_{2}^{-n-1}]}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{[\mu q_{1}^{i}]}{[q_{1}^{i}]}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{[\mu q_{2}^{j}]}{[q_{2}^{j}]},

where we simply denoted the iterative residue as

\displaystyle\underset{\phi=\phi_{[m,n]}}{\Res}=\underset{\phi_{m+n+1}=\mathfrak{a}-n\epsilon_{2}}{\Res}\cdots\underset{\phi_{m+1}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}}{\Res}\underset{\phi_{m}=\mathfrak{a}-m\epsilon_{1}}{\Res}\cdots\underset{\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}}{\Res}.

(4.37)

Note that this formula is also applicable to
m=n=0
. The PT4 vertex is then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
