{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 4.48 L 4.48 4.48 L 4.48 0 Z M 4.48 4.48 M 0 4.48 L 1.73 6.21 L 6.21 6.21 L 4.48 4.48 M 4.48 0 L 6.21 1.73 L 6.21 6.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J})=\frac{h_{2}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a},\phi_{3})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{3}-\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{1})}

(4.97)

and thus the pole is non-zero. After a similar analysis, one will see that poles such as
\phi_{3}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{12,13,14}
disappear.

Summarizing, the poles giving non-zero JK-residues at level three are

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}),\quad(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}),\quad(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{a},\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{a})\,\,(a\in\underline{\textbf{4}}).

(4.98)

5
PT4 counting with surface boundary conditions

Using the contour integrand in the DT surface counting, we can set the
\eta=(-1,-1,\ldots,-1)
and evaluate it. This gives the PT surface counting. We explicitly perform computations for some examples up to three surfaces. Generalizations to situations when we have four, five, and six surfaces is straight forward, but we omit the explicit analysis.

5.1
One surface

Using the symmetry, we can focus on the case when the surfaces span the 12-plane.
Let us first consider the case when the surface boundary conditions are

\displaystyle\lambda_{12}=1,\quad\lambda_{A}=\varnothing\quad(A\neq 12)

(5.1)

and the minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_onesurface_1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(5.2)

The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{34})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4})}.

(5.3)

Obviously for this case, choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
picks no pole and thus the PT4 partition function is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.4)

Actually, for the general case when we have multiple D4
12
-branes, the PT4 partition function is always trivial. For the case

\displaystyle\lambda_{12}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\lambda_{12})}\sum_{j=1}^{\lambda_{12,i}}q_{4}^{i-1}q_{3}^{j-1}

(5.5)

where we have
\lambda_{12,i}
surfaces for each layer in the 4-direction. The framing node is computed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\lambda_{12})}\prod_{j=1}^{\lambda_{12,i}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(i-1)\epsilon_{4}+(j-1)\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})

(5.6)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})\frac{\prod\limits_{(i,j)\in R(\lambda_{12})}\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-i\epsilon_{4}-j\epsilon_{3})}{\prod\limits_{(i,j)\in A(\lambda_{12})}\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-(i-1)\epsilon_{4}-(j-1)\epsilon_{3})},

where
A(\lambda_{12}),R(\lambda_{12})
are the addable and removable boxes of the Young diagram, respectively. The denominator is identified with the addable boxes of the minimal solid partition. Thus, all of the poles are picked up when the reference vector is
\eta=\eta_{0}
and no poles are picked up when
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

When only one of the surface boundary conditions is non-trivial, the PT4 partition function is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.7)

5.2
Two surfaces

Let us move on to the situation when we have two surfaces. Using the symmetry, we can choose one of the surfaces to be the
12
-surface. We have two situations depending on the type of the other surface.

•

Case 1:
The intersection of the two surfaces is a stack of one-dimensional objects:

\displaystyle\lambda_{23},\,\,\lambda_{31},\,\,\lambda_{14},\,\,\lambda_{24}.

(5.8)

For this situation, we say that the intersection is one-dimensional. We call this
D2-brane like
, shortly D2-like.

•

Case 2:
The intersection of the two surfaces is a stack of zero-dimensional objects:

\displaystyle\lambda_{34}.

(5.9)

For this situation, we say that the intersection is zero-dimensional. We call this
D0-brane like
, shortly D0-like.

For case 1, using the quadrality symmetry again, we can focus on the pairs of surfaces
(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23})
. Let us denote the Young diagrams as

\displaystyle\lambda_{12}=(\lambda_{12,1},\lambda_{12,2},\ldots,),\quad\lambda_{23}=(\lambda_{23,1},\lambda_{23,2},\ldots)

(5.10)

where
\lambda_{12,l},\lambda_{23,l}
are the numbers of the 12, 34-surfaces at the
l
-th layer in the fourth direction. The minimal solid partition of such situation is illustrated as

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_twosurface_1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(5.11)

For each layer, the the intersection of the 12, 23 surfaces is a one-dimensional object extending in the 2-direction. More specifically, they are one-dimensional rows of boxes with coordinates

\displaystyle(i-1)\epsilon_{1}+(k-1)\epsilon_{3},\quad 1\leq i\leq\lambda_{23,l},\quad 1\leq k\leq\lambda_{12,l}.

(5.12)

For other choices of surfaces, the illustration might change due to the
(1,3)
decomposition but one can pick a nice frame where the figures look like the one above.

For case 2, the minimal solid partition is illustrated as

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_twosurface_2.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.13)

For each layer, we have
\lambda_{12,l}\,\,(l=1,2,\ldots)
surfaces spanning the 12-surface and a leg boundary condition
\lambda_{34}
extending in the 3-direction. The intersection are then stack of boxes whose coordinates are

\displaystyle(i-1)\epsilon_{1}+(j-1)\epsilon_{2}+(k-1)\epsilon_{3}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4},\quad(i,j)\in\lambda_{34},\quad(k,l)\in\lambda_{12}.

(5.14)

This is the reason why we are calling it a zero-dimensional intersection. Note that we only have
finite
number of such boxes. In particular, we have
|\lambda_{12}|\times|\lambda_{34}|
boxes.

5.2.1
Case 1

Let us study the PT4 partition function of the case 1. The framing node is determined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

(5.15)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_{12,l}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+(k-1)\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\prod_{i=1}^{\lambda_{23,l}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{23}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+(i-1)\epsilon_{1}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\right),

where we shifted the coordinates of the
23
-surfaces to avoid the one-dimensional intersection. Using the infinite product expression of the D8-brane (
2.35
), we can rewrite the framing node contribution as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+N\epsilon_{4},\phi_{I})

(5.16)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{l=1}^{N}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+\lambda_{23,l}\epsilon_{1}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-N\epsilon_{4})}\prod_{l=1}^{N}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-l\epsilon_{4}-\lambda_{23,l}\,\epsilon_{1}-\lambda_{12,l}\,\epsilon_{3})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-(l-1)\epsilon_{4}-\lambda_{23,l}\,\epsilon_{1}-\lambda_{12,l}\,\epsilon_{3})}

where
N=\max\left(\ell(\lambda_{12}),\ell(\lambda_{23})\right)
and we used the (
3.24
) to simplify the expression.

Looking at the poles, one can see that all of the poles are positions of the addable boxes of the minimal solid partition. Thus, choosing the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
picks up all the poles in the denominator, while choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
picks no poles. Therefore, similar to the one-surface case in section
5.1
the PT4 partition function is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.17)

Actually, one can interpret the expression (
5.16
) as a stack of
N
D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-branes and a pair of D8-D8
′
brane with no nontrivial boundary conditions but the Coulomb branch parameters are tuned in a specific way. Since the magnificent four partition function does not depend on the Coulomb branch parameters, using the PE-formula in (
2.36
), the DT partition function after choosing the reference vector
\eta_{0}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]

\displaystyle=\text{PE}\left[\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{[(\mu q_{4}^{-N})q_{4}^{N}]}{[\mathfrak{q}(\mu^{1/2}q_{4}^{-N/2})q_{4}^{N/2}][\mathfrak{q}(\mu^{-1/2}q_{4}^{N/2})q_{4}^{-N/2}]}\right]

(5.18)

\displaystyle=\text{PE}\left[\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{[\mu]}{[\mathfrak{q}\mu^{1/2}][\mathfrak{q}\mu^{-1/2}]}\right]=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]

where we used
\mu=e^{\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a}}
. We have
N+1
pairs of D8-D8
′
branes and
N
of them are reduced to D6-branes after tuning the distance of the D8-D8
′
-branes and tachyon condensation. The D6-branes contributions are
q_{4}^{N}
in the PE formula, while the remaining pair of D8-branes gives
\mu q_{4}^{-N}=e^{\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4}N}
. The result is just the rank one magnificent four partition function.

Due to the triviality of the PT4 partition function, one can observe the following relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}].

(5.19)

Actually, this is the consequence of the DT/PT correspondence of surface counting.

Note that although we did not mention in the previous section, the one-surface case is also included in this situation and the DT/PT correspondence is trivially satisfied.

Proposition 5.2
.

When only two of the surface boundary conditions are nontrivial and they share a one-dimensional intersection, the PT4 partition function is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.20)

5.2.2
Case 2

For the case 2, the framing node is determined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

(5.21)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_{12,l}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+(k-1)\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda_{34}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}2}_{3}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(i-1)\epsilon_{1}+(j-1)\epsilon_{2}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3})\right),

where we decomposed the 34-surface into one-dimensional objects extending in the 3-direction. Again using the D8-D6 relation and the D6-D4 relation, we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\varnothing\,\varnothing\,\lambda_{34}}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I}).

(5.22)

Namely, each layer is understood as a minimal plane partition with a one-leg boundary condition but the origin is shifted by
\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3}
. Thus, the PT4 counting of this situation should be understood as multiple layers of one-leg PT3 counting whose origin is shifted in a specific way. Moreover, similar to the leg boundary condition cases, the boxes are stacked in way such that the gravity points the
(1,1,1,1)
-direction.

To make the discussion more concrete, let us first study the following examples. Such examples were originally studied in
Bae:2022pif
;
Bae:2024bpx
.

Example 1:
\lambda_{12}=\lambda_{34}=1

The minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/surface_ex1.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.23)

The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{a})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{13,23,14,24})}

(5.24)

Choosing the eta vector as
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, the poles picked are

\displaystyle-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{a}=0,\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}=-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(5.25)

For the one instanton case, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}.

(5.26)

For the next level, the poles are chosen as

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(5.27)

All of the poles are canceled by the numerator of the framing node contribution

\displaystyle\prod_{I}\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})

(5.28)

and thus the only nontrivial residue is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[1]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\underset{\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}}{\Res}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\phi_{I}+\mathfrak{a})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{13,23,14,24})}

(5.29)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{13,23,14,24})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\epsilon_{12})}{\operatorname{sh}(\epsilon_{13})\operatorname{sh}(\epsilon_{23})}=\frac{[\mu][q_{12}]}{[q_{13}][q_{23}]}.

We finally have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1+\mathfrak{q}\frac{[\mu][q_{12}]}{[q_{13}][q_{23}]},

(5.30)

which indeed matches with
(
Bae:2024bpx
, Example 1.9, 6.18.(1))
. This PT vertex was called the PT
0
vertex there.

An observation here is that the poles picked up from the JK-residue formalism is the poles of the boxes living the intersection of the two surfaces. For this case, we only have one box at
\mathfrak{a}
. Moreover, the PT4 partition function terminates and we only have finite number of terms.

Example 2:
\lambda_{12}=1+q_{3},\,\lambda_{34}=1

The minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/surface_ex3.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.31)

The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{b}-\phi_{I})}{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}-\phi_{I})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1,2})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{3})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{14})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{24})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{13^{2}})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{23^{2}})}

(5.32)

With
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, the poles are

\displaystyle\phi_{I}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}=-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(5.33)

For the first level, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}

(5.34)

giving the residue

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[1]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\underset{\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}}{\Res}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{1})=\frac{[q_{3}^{-1}\mu][q_{12}][q_{3}^{2}]}{[q_{3}][q_{1}q_{3}^{2}][q_{2}q_{3}^{2}]}.

(5.35)

For the second level, we have the poles

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{1,2,4}.

(5.36)

The poles
\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{1,2,4}
are canceled by the numerator

\displaystyle\prod_{I=1}^{k}\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{1,2,4})

(5.37)

and the non-zero JK-residue is evaluated as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[2]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{2}\underset{\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}}{\Res}\,\underset{\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}}{\Res}\prod_{I=1}^{2}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})

(5.38)

\displaystyle=\frac{[\mu][q_{3}^{-1}\mu][q_{12}][q_{12}q_{3}^{-1}]}{[q_{13}][q_{23}][q_{1}q_{3}^{2}][q_{2}q_{3}^{2}]}.

Therefore, the PT4 partition function is given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1+\mathfrak{q}\frac{[q_{3}^{-1}\mu][q_{12}][q_{3}^{2}]}{[q_{3}][q_{1}q_{3}^{2}][q_{2}q_{3}^{2}]}+\mathfrak{q}^{2}\frac{[\mu][q_{3}^{-1}\mu][q_{12}][q_{12}q_{3}^{-1}]}{[q_{13}][q_{23}][q_{1}q_{3}^{2}][q_{2}q_{3}^{2}]},

(5.39)

which indeed matches with
(
Bae:2024bpx
, Example 6.18.(3))
.

Similar to the previous case, the poles at
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}
belong to the intersection of the two surfaces and the PT4 partition function terminates at level two, eventually giving only finite number of terms. Moreover, for this case, we have a
melting rule
such that we can place a box at
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}
and then place a box at
\mathfrak{a}
. This is interpreted as a part of the PT3 counting rule with one leg extending at the three direction whose origin is
\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{3}
.

Example 3:
\lambda_{12}=1+q_{3}+q_{4},\lambda_{34}=1

The minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_twosurface_3.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.40)

The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})=\frac{\operatorname{sh}\left(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}{\operatorname{sh}\left(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}-\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}-\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)}

(5.41)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)}{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}.

Choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, for level one, the poles picked up are

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\quad\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}.

(5.42)

Let us next consider the next level. Assume we picked up the pole at
\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}
. For the next level, we can choose

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\,\,\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4},\quad\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(5.43)

The pole
\phi_{2}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}=\phi_{1}
is canceled by the numerator of the D0-brane contribution. The poles
\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{1,2,4}
are all canceled by the numerators of the framing node contribution. We thus have

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}).

(5.44)

Starting from
\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}
also gives the same configuration.

For level three, the following poles are picked

\displaystyle\phi_{3}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4},\quad\phi_{2}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(5.45)

One will see that all of the poles except
\phi_{3}=\mathfrak{a}
vanish. We thus have

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3})=(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4},\mathfrak{a}).

(5.46)

For higher levels, similar to the previous examples, the partition function vanishes and thus we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1+\mathfrak{q}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{3})}+\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{4})}\right)+\mathfrak{q}^{2}\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4})}+\mathfrak{q}^{3}\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4},0)},

(5.47)

where we denoted the JK-residue of the integrand without the Weyl group factor at
\mathfrak{a}+x,\mathfrak{a}+y,\mathfrak{a}+z
as
\mathcal{Z}_{(x,y,z)}
.

Let us interpret the results. The poles
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}
correspond to the boxes of the PT3 counting for layer
1,2
, respectively. For layer one, since the origin is shifted to
\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{3}
, if one performs the PT3 counting, the first box is
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}
. For layer two, due to the shift
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{3}
, the first box is
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}
. Choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
imposes the condition that the gravity is pointing the
(1,1,1,1)
direction and thus boxes need to be supported from the positive direction. Since both boxes are supported, we can place both of them at level one.

Let us consider the next level. Assume we have a box at
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3}
. We trivially can place the boxes at
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}
, because it does not break the melting rule. Following the PT3 counting rules, one would expect that we can place a box at
\mathfrak{a}
. However, this is not allowed, because it is not supported by any box from the positive direction of the 4-axis.

Similarly, assume we have a box at
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}
. We cannot place a box at
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{3}
because it is not supported from the positive direction. Accordingly, we cannot place any boxes at the layer
l\geq 3
.

For level three, we can only additionally place a box at
\mathfrak{a}
, which does not break the melting rule. The allowed configuration is then
(\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4},0)
.

For higher levels, one would expect we can add a boxes at positions with negative coordinates of the 3-axis. However, such boxes are not supported from the positive direction of the 4-axis and thus they are not allowed. Therefore, we do not have any configurations for higher levels.

General case

Given the above three examples, we can already deduce the PT4 box-counting rules for the situation when we have two surfaces with zero-dimensional intersection. The rules are as follows.

Proposition 5.3
.

When only two of the surfaces are nontrivial, and the intersection of them is zero-dimensional, the PT4 box counting rules and the PT4 partition function are given as follows.

•

We first start from the minimal solid partition in (
5.13
). As mentioned, each layer is understood as a minimal plane partition with one-leg boundary conditions whose origin is shifted
\lambda_{12,l}
in the positive direction of the 3-axis.

•

Similar to usual PT3 counting, we then extend the one-leg boundary conditions to the negative direction and then remove the boxes at the positive quadrant. Since the origin is shifted, we obtain

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_twosurface_rule1.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.48)

Such boxes give the hollow structure in which the boxes are placed.

•

Boxes are stacked inside the hollow structure in a way such that the gravity is pointing
(1,1,1,1)
. Namely, boxes are supported from the positive directions.

•

Since the surfaces
\lambda_{34}
extends infinitely in the 4-direction, while the surfaces
\lambda_{12}
terminate at
\ell(\lambda_{12})
in the 4-direction, we can not place boxes at layers
l\geq\ell(\lambda_{12})+1
. This is because, due to the previous melting rule, the boxes also need to be supported from the positive direction of the 4-axis. So that we can place a box at the layer
\ell(\lambda_{12})+1
, we also need a box at layer
\ell(\lambda_{12})+2
, which inductively means we need infinite number of boxes extending in the 4-direction.

We thus can only place boxes at the intersection of the two surfaces (
5.14
) (the pink positions):

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_twosurface_rule2.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.49)

•

The maximal number of boxes we can add is
|\lambda_{12}|\times|\lambda_{34}|
and thus the PT4 partition function terminates at level
|\lambda_{12}|\times|\lambda_{34}|
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{|\lambda_{12}|\times|\lambda_{34}|}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[k].

(5.50)

5.3
Three surfaces

Fixing one of the surface to be spanning the 12-plane, we have 10 possible cases with three surfaces. They are classified in the following three situations.

•

Case 1:
All of the intersections are D2-like and the surfaces can be embedded in a
\mathbb{C}^{3}
subspace:

\displaystyle(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23},\lambda_{31}),\quad(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{41},\lambda_{42}).

(5.51)

•

Case 2:
All of the intersections are D2-like but the union of the surfaces span
\mathbb{C}^{4}
:

\displaystyle(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23},\lambda_{42}),\quad(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{31},\lambda_{41}).

(5.52)

•

Case 3:
One of the three intersections is a D0-like intersection. Namely, two out of the three surfaces are transverse with each other:

\displaystyle(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23},\lambda_{41}),\quad(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23},\lambda_{34}),\quad(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{31},\lambda_{34}),

(5.53)

\displaystyle(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{31},\lambda_{24}),\quad(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{41},\lambda_{34}),\quad(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{24},\lambda_{34})

5.3.1
Case 1

Using the symmetry we can focus on the three surfaces
(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23},\lambda_{31})
, whose minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_threesurface_1.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(5.54)

We denote the Young diagrams as

\displaystyle\lambda_{12}=(\lambda_{12,1},\ldots),\quad\lambda_{23}=(\lambda_{23,1},\lambda_{23,2},\ldots),\quad\lambda_{31}=(\lambda_{31,1},\lambda_{31,2},\ldots)

(5.55)

where
\lambda_{12,l},\lambda_{23,l},\lambda_{31,l}
are the numbers of surfaces for each layer.

The framing node contribution is computed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

(5.56)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{\lambda_{12,l}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{12}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+(k-1)\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\right.\prod_{i=1}^{\lambda_{23,l}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{23}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+(i-1)\epsilon_{1}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle\times\left.\prod_{j=1}^{\lambda_{13,l}}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}4}_{31}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3}+\lambda_{23,l}\epsilon_{1}+(j-1)\epsilon_{2},\phi_{I})\right).

Similar to the case 1 of the two-surfaces case, using the infinite product expression of the D8-brane, we can rewrite it as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+N\epsilon_{4},\phi_{I})

(5.57)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{l=1}^{N}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+\lambda_{23,l}\epsilon_{1}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3}+\lambda_{31,l}\epsilon_{2},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-N\epsilon_{4})}\prod_{l=1}^{N}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-l\epsilon_{4}-\lambda_{23,l}\,\epsilon_{1}-\lambda_{12,l}\,\epsilon_{3}-\lambda_{31,l}\epsilon_{2})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-(l-1)\epsilon_{4}-\lambda_{23,l}\,\epsilon_{1}-\lambda_{12,l}\,\epsilon_{3}-\lambda_{31,l}\epsilon_{2})}

where
N=\max(\ell(\lambda_{12}),\ell(\lambda_{23}),\ell(\lambda_{31}))
. Note that again all of the poles correspond to the addable boxes of the minimal solid partition.

The framing node implies that we can understand this setup as a stack of D8-D8
′
brane and
N
D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-branes with their Coulomb branch parameters tuned. Picking the reference vector as
\eta=\eta_{0}
and performing the JK-residue gives the following DT4 partition function

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]

\displaystyle=\text{PE}\left[\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}\frac{[(\mu q_{4}^{-N})q_{4}^{N}]}{[\mathfrak{q}(\mu^{1/2}q_{4}^{-N/2})q_{4}^{N/2}][\mathfrak{q}(\mu^{-1/2}q_{4}^{N/2})q_{4}^{-N/2}]}\right]

(5.58)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]

which is similar to section
5.2.1
.

Moreover, since choosing the reference vector to be
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
picks no poles, the PT partition function is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.59)

5.3.2
Case 2

Let us then move on to the situation when the union of surfaces span
\mathbb{C}^{4}
. We focus on
(\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23},\lambda_{42})
whose minimal solid partition looks like

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_threesurface_2.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.60)

To make the discussion concrete, let us focus on the example in the figure with

\displaystyle\lambda_{12}=(2,1,1),\quad\lambda_{23}=(2,1),\quad\lambda_{42}=(2,1,1).

(5.61)

The framing node contribution of this actually gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1^{2}34^{3}})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{13^{3}4^{2}})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{4})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{3})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{1}-3\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-3\epsilon_{3}-2\epsilon_{4})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{134})}

(5.62)

where we used
\epsilon_{1^{a}2^{b}3^{c}4^{d}}=a\epsilon_{1}+b\epsilon_{2}+c\epsilon_{3}+d\epsilon_{4}
. Obviously all of the poles correspond to the position of the addable boxes and so after choosing the reference vector to be
\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, no poles are picked up and the PT4 partition function is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.63)

If we choose the reference vector to be
\eta=\eta_{0}
instead, the DT4 partition actually becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}].

(5.64)

One way to see this is to observe that we have the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+3\epsilon_{4}+2\epsilon_{1},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

(5.65)

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{1}+2\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{1}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{4}+3\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{1}}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}+3\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I}).

Here we have a system with three D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-branes, two D6
{}_{\bar{1}}
-branes and a pair of D8-D8
′
brane with the distance
\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a}-3\epsilon_{4}-2\epsilon_{1}
, which is a tetrahedron instanton system coupled with a pair of D8-branes. The total central charge is

\displaystyle(\mu q_{4}^{-3}q_{1}^{-2})\times q_{4}^{3}\times q_{1}^{2}=\mu

(5.66)

and thus we obtain the magnificent four partition function.

Let us give a more intuitive way to see the decomposition into D6 and D8-branes. The D8-brane is understood as a positive quadrant of a four-dimensional region. In the
(1,3)
decomposition, the D6-branes are understood as the positive quadrant of three-dimensional spaces in the four-dimensional space where we can stack boxes (D0-branes). In particular, the D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-branes are understood as a three-dimensional cube spanning the 123-space:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_D64bar-brane.pdf}}}}}}}$}

(5.67)

On the other hand, other D6
{}_{\bar{1,2,3}}
-branes are understood as two-dimensional surfaces extending semi-infinitely in the positive direction of the 4-axis:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_D61bar-brane.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.68)

where this is an example of a D6
{}_{\bar{1}}
-brane. The smallest coordinate in the orange region represents the Coulomb branch parameter of the D6, D8-branes.

Given a minimal solid partition, the complement of it inside the positive quadrant of the four-dimensional region is the positions where we can add boxes. If such region can be decomposed into collection of D6 and D8-branes, the DT4 partition function is computable using the PE formula (
2.36
). The information we need to do so is the number of the D6-branes and the distance between the D8-D8
′
branes.

For the example above, it is obvious that the complement for the first layer can be filled in by a
D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-brane whose origin is
\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{1}+2\epsilon_{3}
. For the second layer, we can use a D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-brane with the origin
\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3}
. For the higher layers, we need to be careful with the
\lambda_{24}
-surface. For the third layer, a candidate to fill the complement is to first place a D6
{}_{\bar{1}}
-brane at
\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{4}+3\epsilon_{3}
which fills the region
x_{1}=1,x_{2}\geq 1,x_{3}\geq 3,x_{4}\geq 3
. The remaining regions in the third layer can then be filled by placing a D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-brane at
\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3}
. For higher layers, note that the D6
{}_{\bar{1}}
-placed at the third layer also fills a part of the complement. The remaining regions can be filled by placing another D6
{}_{\bar{1}}
-brane at
\mathfrak{a}+2\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{3}
that fills the region
x_{1}=2,x_{2}\geq 1,x_{3}\geq 2,x_{4}\geq 3
and a D8-brane at
\mathfrak{a}+3\epsilon_{4}+2\epsilon_{1}
. In this way, we can observe the decomposition (
5.65
).

This phenomenon is actually general for generic
\lambda_{12},\lambda_{23},\lambda_{24}
and the DT4 partition function is always the rank one magnificent four partition function.

The triviality of the PT4 partition function can be also observed in a more intuitive way. Using the symmetry, let us instead consider the surfaces
\lambda_{14},\lambda_{24},\lambda_{34}
:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_threesurface_3.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.69)

Each layer is understood as a minimal plane partition with
\lambda_{14},\lambda_{24},\lambda_{34}
as boundary conditions and they are extending semi-infinitely in the positive direction of the 4-axis. We then use the standard prescription to obtain the PT3 counting for each layer:

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_threesurface_4.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.70)

However, since now the boxes need to be supported also from the positive direction of the 4-axis, we cannot place any boxes in the hollow structure above. Thus, the PT4 partition function must be trivial.

Combining with the previous situation (case 1), we have the following proposition.

Proposition 5.4
.

When only three of the surface boundary conditions are nontrivial and all of the intersections are D2-like, the PT4 partition function is trivial:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.71)

5.3.3
Case 3

Let us then move on to the case when one of the three intersections is a D0-like intersection. Using the symmetry, we choose the three surfaces
\lambda_{12},\lambda_{13},\lambda_{34}
. The intersection of the 12 and 34 surfaces are the D0-like. For this case, there are situations when the PT4 partition function is nontrivial.

To make the discussion concrete, let us consider the following examples.

Example 1:
\lambda_{12}=1+q_{3}+q_{4},\,\lambda_{13}=1+q_{4},\,\lambda_{34}=1

The minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_threesurface_5.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.72)

The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}\left(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}

(5.73)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}.

Choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, obviously no poles are picked up and so the partition function is trivial

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1.

(5.74)

Example 2:
\lambda_{12}=1+q_{4},\,\lambda_{13}=\lambda_{34}=1

The minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/surface_ex2.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.75)

The framing node contribution is computed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{b}-\phi_{I})}{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}-\phi_{I})}\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1,2,3})}{\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{134})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{23})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{14^{2}})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{24^{2}})}.

(5.76)

Choosing the reference vector as
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, the poles picked up are

\displaystyle\phi_{I}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4},\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}=-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(5.77)

For the first level, we can take

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}.

(5.78)

For next level, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}=\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{1,2,3}

(5.79)

but all of them cancel with the numerator

\displaystyle\prod_{I=1}^{k}\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a})\operatorname{sh}(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{1,2,3}).

(5.80)

Therefore, the only nontrivial JK-residue is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[1]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\underset{\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}}{\Res}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{1})

(5.81)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}(\epsilon_{12})\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{123})}{\operatorname{sh}(\epsilon_{13})\operatorname{sh}(\epsilon_{1}+2\epsilon_{2}+2\epsilon_{3})}=\frac{[q_{123}\mu][q_{12}]}{[q_{13}][q_{1}q_{2}^{2}q_{3}^{2}]},

which gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1+\mathfrak{q}\frac{[q_{123}\mu][q_{12}]}{[q_{13}][q_{1}q_{2}^{2}q_{3}^{2}]}.

(5.82)

This indeed matches with
(
Bae:2024bpx
, Example 6.18.(2))
.

Example 3:
\lambda_{12}=1+q_{3}+q_{4},\,\lambda_{13}=1+q_{4},\,\lambda_{34}=1+q_{2}

The minimal solid partition is

\displaystyle\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{PT4Figures/PT4_threesurface_6.pdf}}}}}}}$}\cdots

(5.83)

and the corresponding framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{\mathfrak{b}},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{sh}\left(\phi_{I}-\mathfrak{b}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{2}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)}{\operatorname{sh}\left(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}-\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{4}-\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}

(5.84)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{1}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}{\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{2}-2\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)\operatorname{sh}\left(-\mathfrak{a}-2\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}+\phi_{I}\right)}.

Note that the pole at
\phi_{I}=\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1234}
is understood as the addable pole of the minimal solid partition.

We can perform the JK-residue formalism explicitly as the previous example. Let us only list down the poles giving non-zero JK-residues. At level one, the poles picked up are

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{23},\quad\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{24}.

(5.85)

For level two, the poles picked up are

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})=(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{23},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{24}).

(5.86)

For level three, we have

\displaystyle(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3})

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{23},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{24},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2}).

(5.87)

For higher levels, the residue vanishes and thus the PT4 partition function terminates at finite terms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=1+\mathfrak{q}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{23})}+\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{24})}\right)+\mathfrak{q}^{2}\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{23},\epsilon_{24})}+\mathfrak{q}^{3}\mathcal{Z}_{(\epsilon_{23},\epsilon_{24},\epsilon_{2})}.

(5.88)

General case

The above examples have an intuitive combinatorial structure which is just a generalization of Prop.
5.3
. The difference is the existence of the 13-surface and that the D0-like intersections coming from the 12 and 34-surfaces may be included in it. For Example 1, the intersections of the 12, 34 surfaces are
\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}\}
and all of them also belong to the 13-surface. For Example 2, the intersections of the 12, 34 surfaces are
\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4}\}
and
\mathfrak{a}
is included in the 13-surface. For Example 3, the D0-like intersections are
\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{3},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{23},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{4},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{34},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{24}\}
and only
\{\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{23},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{24},\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{2}\}
are not included in the 13-surface. Looking at the results of the non-zero JK-residues, one can observe that the D0-like intersections that do not belong to the 13-surface only appears.

This phenomenon can be understood easily by decomposing the minimal solid partition to an infinite sequence of minimal plane partitions with boundary conditions. We denote the 12,13-surfaces as
\lambda_{12}=(\lambda_{12,l}),\,\,\lambda_{13}=(\lambda_{13,l})
, where again each component corresponds to the number of surfaces at layer
l
. The Young diagram
\lambda_{34}
is a constant Young diagram for each layer. Due to the existence of the 13-surface, we can construct a Young diagram
\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34}
for each layer that do not have any intersections with the 13-surface:

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34}=(\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,1},\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,2},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,i},\ldots),\quad\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,i}=\max(\lambda_{34,1}^{(l)}-\lambda_{13,l},0).

(5.89)

In other words, we are cutting of from the original Young diagram
\lambda_{34}
the part including the 13-surface. Each layer then can be understood as a minimal plane partition with one-leg boundary condition
\tilde{\lambda}_{34}^{(l)}
whose origin is
\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+\lambda_{13,l}\epsilon_{2}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3}
. Indeed the framing node contribution obeys

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{\mathfrak{b}},\phi_{I})=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}6_{\bar{4}}\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\varnothing\varnothing\tilde{\lambda}_{34}^{(l)}}(\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+\lambda_{13,l}\epsilon_{2}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3},\phi_{I}).

(5.90)

We then can use the standard prescription to obtain the PT3 configurations for each layers, but additionally we have the gravity pointing the
(1,1,1,1)
-direction (see Cond.
4.1
). We extend the minimal plane partition to the negative direction and then remove the positive quadrant boxes and obtain a hollow structure extending in the negative direction. Boxes are stacked in it by obeying the melting rule.

Similar to Prop.
5.3
, the PT configurations terminate at some level because for suitable large
l
, we cannot place any boxes at the positions
\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}-\epsilon_{3}k
for
k>0
. Thus, the PT4 partition function terminates.

Proposition 5.5
.

When only three of the surfaces are nontrivial and one of the three intersections is D0-like, the PT4 box counting rules and the PT4 partition function are given as follows. Without loosing generality, we consider the case when
\lambda_{12,34,13}
are nontrivial.

•

We first start from the minimal solid partition. We can define a sequence of Young diagrams
\tilde{\lambda}_{34}^{(l)}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34}=(\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,1},\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,2},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,i},\ldots),\quad\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34,i}=\max(\lambda_{34,1}^{(l)}-\lambda_{13,l},0)

(5.91)

In the
(1,3)
-description, each layer is understood as a minimal plane partition with one-leg
\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34}
whose origin is shifted by the 12, 13-surfaces to
\mathfrak{a}+(l-1)\epsilon_{4}+\lambda_{13,l}\epsilon_{2}+\lambda_{12,l}\epsilon_{3}
.

•

Similar to usual PT3 counting, we then extend the one-leg boundary conditions to the negative direction and then remove the boxes at the positive quadrant. Such boxes give the hollow structure in which the boxes are placed.

•

Boxes are stacked inside the hollow structure in a way such that the gravity is pointing
(1,1,1,1)
. Namely, boxes are supported from the positive directions.

•

Boxes can be stacked only at the positions

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}+\epsilon_{1}(i-1)+\epsilon_{2}(j-1)+\epsilon_{3}(k-1)+\epsilon_{4}(l-1),\quad(i,j)\in\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34},\,\,1\leq k\leq\lambda_{12,l}.

(5.92)

•

The maximal number of boxes we can add is
\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{12,l}|\times|\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34}|
, which is finite and thus the PT4 partition function terminates at this level

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[k],\quad N=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{12,l}|\times|\tilde{\lambda}^{(l)}_{34}|.

(5.93)

6
DT/PT correspondence

The equivariant DT4 vertex and PT4 vertex are related by an overall factor coming from the magnificent four partition function. Such phenomenon is called the DT/PT correspondence
Pandharipande:2007sq
;
Pandharipande:2007kc
;
Toda:2008ASPM
;
Toda:2010JAMS
;
Bridgeland:2011JAMS
;
Stoppa:2011BSMF
;
Toda2016HallAI
;
Kononov:2019fni
;
Jenne2020TheCP
;
Jenne:2021irh
;
Kuhn:2023koa
;
Cao:2014bca
;
Cao:2014bca
;
Cao:2019tnw
;
Cao:2019tvv
;
liu20234foldpandharipandethomasvertex
;
Bae:2024bpx
.

In the JK-residue formalism, the computations using either
\eta=\eta_{0}
or
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
are related with each other. We have confirmed them for various examples up to three-instantons. We note that although we call all the statements as theorems, they should be understood as conjectures for the moment.

6.1
Rank one DT/PT correspondence

The DT/PT correspondence was already observed when the PT partition functions are trivial in the one, two, three surfaces cases (see for example (
5.19
)). Even when the partition function is nontrivial, for both the leg and surface boundary conditions, we have the following DT/PT correspondence in our notations.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\pi_{1,2,3,4}
be plane partitions and then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}[\mu]\,\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}].

(6.1)

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\{\lambda_{A}\}_{A\in\underline{\textbf{6}}}
be Young diagrams and then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}[\mu]\,\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},\mu;q_{1,2,3,4}]

(6.2)

We note that although we do not discuss in this paper, one can take specific limits of
\mu
to decouple the parameter
\mu
and we still have the DT/PT correspondence. In this paper, we always keep the parameter
\mu
.

Wall-crossing

From the JK-residue viewpoint, the fact that the result depends on the reference vector is a consequence of the wall-crossing phenomenon
Hori:2014tda
. In the contour integral formalism, residues at the asymptotics are the origin of it. We leave a detailed discussion for future work but let us briefly discuss it for the one-leg one box case at one instanton level. See
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
for a similar discussion for the PT3 case.

Consider the integral

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{d\phi}{2\pi i}\mathcal{Z}_{\varnothing\varnothing\varnothing\,\hbox to4.89pt{\vbox to4.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 4.48 L 4.48 4.48 L 4.48 0 Z M 4.48 4.48 M 0 4.48 L 1.73 6.21 L 6.21 6.21 L 4.48 4.48 M 4.48 0 L 6.21 1.73 L 6.21 6.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi)

(6.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-q_{12})(1-q_{13})(1-q_{23})}{(1-q_{1})(1-q_{2})(1-q_{3})(1-q_{123})}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}\mu^{1/2}\frac{(z-\mu^{-1})(z-q_{12,13,23})}{(z-q_{4}^{-1})(z-q_{1,2,3})}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}g(z)

where in the second line we used the multiplicative notation and
e^{\phi-\mathfrak{a}}=z,e^{\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{a}}=\mu
and
\mathcal{C}
is some contour traversed counter-clockwise. Since we have the condition
q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}=1
, we need to be careful with the analytic region and at least one of the parameters need to obey
|q_{a}|>1
. Let us assume that the parameters obey
|q_{1,2,3}|<|q_{4}^{-1}|<1
and that the contour
\mathcal{C}
contains
q_{1,2,3}
but not
q_{4}^{-1}
.

Taking the poles inside the region surrounded by
\mathcal{C}
gives

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}g(z)

\displaystyle=\underset{z=0}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]+\underset{z=q_{1}}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]+\underset{z=q_{2}}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]+\underset{z=q_{3}}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]

(6.4)

\displaystyle=\underset{z=0}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]+\oint_{\eta_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}g(z).

Picking the poles outside the region instead gives

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}g(z)

\displaystyle=-\underset{z=\infty}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]-\underset{z=q_{4}^{-1}}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]

(6.5)

\displaystyle=-\underset{z=\infty}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]+\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}g(z).

Since the residue is the same, we have

\displaystyle\oint_{{\eta}_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}g(z)-\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}g(z)

\displaystyle=-\underset{z=\infty}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]-\underset{z=0}{\Res}\left[g(z)\frac{dz}{z}\right]

(6.6)

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}g(z)-\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}g(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{[q_{14}][q_{24}][q_{34}][\mu]}{[q_{1}][q_{2}][q_{3}][q_{4}]}

which indeed matches with the one-instanton contribution of the magnificent four.

One can perform this computation and study the asymptotics of the framing node contributions for the leg and surface boundary conditions generally and can confirm that at one-instanton level we always have the DT/PT correspondence.

6.2
Rank
n
DT/PT correspondence

Let us move on to higher rank generalizations, where we have multiple pairs of D8-D8
′
branes in the setup. We assume that we have
n
pairs of D8-D8
′
branes.

Let us start from the leg boundary conditions first. For each pair of D8-D8
′
branes, we can associate a set of plane partitions to each leg, which we denote as
\vec{\pi}_{a}=(\pi_{a}^{(\alpha)})\,(a\in\underline{\textbf{4}})
. The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\pi_{1}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{2}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{3}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{4}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I}).

(6.7)

The DT partition function is then defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[k]

(6.8)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\eta_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\pi_{1}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{2}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{3}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{4}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(6.9)

Choosing the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
, the poles picked up are classified by
n
-tuple of solid partitions with the leg boundary conditions. Generally, it depends on the parameters
\vec{v}=(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{n}=(e^{\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha}})_{\alpha=1}^{n}
and
\vec{w}=(w_{\alpha})_{\alpha=1}^{n}=(e^{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}})_{\alpha=1}^{n}
, but we omit the dependence of it.

On the other hand, the PT partition function is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[k]

(6.10)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\pi_{1}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{2}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{3}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{4}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(6.11)

Choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
, the poles picked up are understood as multiple PT4 configurations. When three and four legs are nontrivial, the integrand contains second or third order poles and thus evaluating the residue includes higher derivatives acting on the integrand. Such derivatives also act on the contribution coming from the other D8-branes and so the evaluation will be complicated.

Even for higher rank DT and PT countings, we have the DT/PT correspondence.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
\vec{\pi}_{a}
be
n
-tuples of plane partitions for the four legs and
\vec{v},\vec{w}
be the flavor fugacities of the
n
D8 and D8
′
-branes. We then have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}\right]\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\vec{\pi}_{1}\vec{\pi}_{2}\vec{\pi}_{3}\vec{\pi}_{4}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}].

(6.12)

For the surface boundary conditions, we denote the surfaces as
\vec{\lambda}_{A}=(\lambda_{A}^{(\alpha)})_{\alpha=1,\ldots,n}
. The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I}).

(6.13)

The DT partition function is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[k]

(6.14)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\eta_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(6.15)

The poles are classified by
n
-tuple of solid partitions with surface boundary conditions.

The PT partition function is defined similarly as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[k]

(6.16)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

(6.17)

We will obtain
n
-tuples of PT4 counting with surface boundary conditions.

The DT/PT correspondence also holds.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\vec{\lambda}_{A}
be
n
-tuples of plane partitions for the six surfaces and
\vec{v},\vec{w}
be the flavor fugacities of the
n
D8 and D8
′
-branes. We then have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{DT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}\right]\mathcal{Z}^{\text{PT}\text{--}\text{JK}}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}].

(6.18)

6.3
Introducing anti-fundamental multiplets

Following the discussion in
(
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
, section 3.8)
, we can also introduce anti-fundamental chiral and Fermi multiplets to the supersymmetric quantum mechanics and compute the Witten index:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})=\frac{1}{\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{a}-\phi_{I})},\quad\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})=\operatorname{sh}(\mathfrak{a}-\phi_{I})

(6.19)

where we denoted the corresponding D8-branes using the notation
\overline{\text{D}8}
,
\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
. The physical implication of this setup is yet to be studied and we will not discuss it in this paper. The quiver diagram for the 1d
\mathcal{N}=2
SQM is written as

\displaystyle\hbox to152.66pt{\vbox to100.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-38.9047pt\lower-44.20766pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
