\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(6.20)

Let us first start from a setup when we do not have the D8-D8
′
branes, which gives the following framing node contribution

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I}).

(6.21)

Choosing the reference vector to be
\eta=\eta_{0}
, no poles are picked up and the partition function is trivial. Instead, choosing
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
picks the poles from the hyperplanes

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}-\phi_{I}=0,\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{J}=\epsilon_{1,2,3,4}.

(6.22)

Recursively, one can show that the poles are classified by solid partitions, but this time, the coordinates are

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}-(i-1)\epsilon_{1}-(j-1)\epsilon_{2}-(k-1)\epsilon_{3}-(l-1)\epsilon_{4}.

(6.23)

Namely, the solid partition extends in the negative direction.

Higher rank generalizations are obtained by including
n
pairs of D8-D8
′
branes and
m
pairs of
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
branes.
5
5

5

One can also consider different number of D8 (
\overline{\text{D}8}
) and D8
′
(
\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
) branes but in this paper we only focus when the numbers of them are the same.
The framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{c}_{\beta},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{d}_{\beta},\phi_{I}).

(6.24)

and the partition function is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,+}_{n|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,+}_{n|m}[k]

(6.25)

where the
k
-instanton sector is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,+}_{n|m}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\eta_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})

(6.26)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{c}_{\beta},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{d}_{\beta},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

The positive sign
+
denotes the fact that the reference vector is chosen to be
\eta=\eta_{0}
. For this case, the JK-residue formalism picks the poles coming from the D8-D8
′
-branes and they are classified by multiple solid partitions extending in the positive direction. The
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
-branes simply play the roles of extra matter contributions.

We can also define a different partition function

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,-}_{n|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,-}_{n|m}[k]

(6.27)

where the
k
-instanton sector is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,-}_{n|m}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})

(6.28)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{c}_{\beta},\phi_{I})\mathcal{Z}^{\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}\text{--}\text{D}0}(\mathfrak{d}_{\beta},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

The negative sign
-
denotes the fact that the reference vector is
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
. This time, the poles picked up come from the
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
-branes and they are classified by multiple solid partitions extending in the negative direction. Note again that for this case, the
\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}8^{\prime}
-branes play the roles of extra matter contributions.

An interesting property is that we also have a “DT/PT correspondence" between these two partition functions.

Theorem 6.5
.

We have the following identity

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,+}_{n|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\frac{y_{\beta}}{x_{\beta}}\right]\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,-}_{n|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]

(6.29)

where

\displaystyle v_{\alpha}=e^{\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha}},\quad w_{\alpha}=e^{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}},\quad x_{\beta}=e^{\mathfrak{c}_{\beta}},\quad y_{\beta}=e^{\mathfrak{d}_{\beta}}.

(6.30)

Corollary 6.5.1
.

When
m=0
, the partition function
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,-}_{n|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]
is trivial and we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,+}_{n|0}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}\right]

(6.31)

which is the PE-formula of the rank
n
magnificent four partition function discussed in
Nekrasov:2018xsb
.

This comes from the fact that when
m=0
, we do not have any anti-fundamental multiplets and thus choosing the reference vector
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
picks no poles and the partition function is trivial.

Corollary 6.5.2
.

When
n=0
, the partition function
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,+}_{n|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]
is trivial and we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,-}_{0|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{m}\frac{x_{\beta}}{y_{\beta}}\right],

(6.32)

which is also the rank
m
magnificent four partition function. Note that the ratio of the flavor fugacities of the
\overline{\text{D}8}
-branes are opposite from that of the previous corollary.

Similar to the previous case, when
n=0
, we do not have any fundamental multiplets and choosing the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
picks no poles and the partition function of the positive part is trivial. From Thm.
6.5
, we have

\displaystyle 1=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{m}\frac{y_{\beta}}{x_{\beta}}\right]\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8,-}_{0|m}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}].

(6.33)

Using the reflection property (
2.40
), we obtain the claim.

If we tune the parameters as

\displaystyle\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}=q_{4},\quad\frac{y_{\beta}}{x_{\beta}}=q_{4}^{-1}

(6.34)

we obtain the pure
U(n|m)
gauge theory obtained from
n
D6
{}_{\bar{4}}
-branes and
m

\overline{\text{D}6}_{\bar{4}}
-branes discussed in
(
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
, section 3.8.3)
. Note that for the
\overline{\text{D}8}
-
\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
branes, the tuning parameters are the inverse. Instead, tuning the parameters as

\displaystyle\left\{\left.\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}\right|\alpha=1,\ldots,n\right\}\rightarrow\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}\},\quad\left\{\left.\frac{y_{\beta}}{x_{\beta}}\right|\beta=1,\ldots,m\right\}\rightarrow\{q_{1}^{-1},q_{2}^{-1},q_{3}^{-1},q_{4}^{-1}\},

(6.35)

we obtain the tetrahedron version of the supergroup theory.

Leg and surface boundary conditions

We can also perform the same generalization for the leg and surface boundary conditions. We start from the leg boundary conditions. We introduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

(6.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\left.\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\pi_{3}\pi_{4}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\epsilon_{a}\rightarrow-\epsilon_{a}\\
\operatorname{sh}(x)\rightarrow-\operatorname{sh}(-x)\end{subarray}}

where the negative part is obtained by reversing the sign of the
\epsilon
-parameters and changing the fundamental/antifundamental multiplets to antifundamental/fundamental multiplets.

We introduce
n
pairs of D8-D8
′
branes and
m
pairs of
\overline{\text{D}8}
-
\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
branes and denote the boundary conditions as
(\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm},\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm},\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm},\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm})
, respectively. Let us consider the framing node contribution

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\pi_{1,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{2,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{3,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{4,+}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\pi_{1,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{2,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{3,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{4,-}^{(\beta)}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\beta},\mathfrak{d}_{\beta},\phi_{I}).

(6.37)

We define the partition function as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[k]

(6.38)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\eta_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\pi_{1,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{2,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{3,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{4,+}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})

(6.39)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\pi_{1,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{2,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{3,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{4,-}^{(\beta)}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\beta},\mathfrak{d}_{\beta},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

Choosing the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
, the poles picked up from the D8-D8
′
part is the rank
n
DT4 counting with
(\vec{\pi}_{1,+},\vec{\pi}_{2,+},\vec{\pi}_{3,+},\vec{\pi}_{4,+})
, while the poles picked up from the
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
part is the PT4 counting with
(\vec{\pi}_{1,-},\vec{\pi}_{2,-},\vec{\pi}_{3,-},\vec{\pi}_{4,-})
. Strictly speaking, the PT4 counting appearing here is the PT4 counting with the coordinates
\epsilon_{a}\rightarrow-\epsilon_{a}
compared to the discussion in the previous sections.
6
6

6

In
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
, this was called the conjugate PT counting.
The arising partition function is a mixed of DT4 and PT4 countings and so we call this the (DT|PT) 4-vertex or the (DT|PT) partition function following
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
.

We can define a different partition function with the reference vector
\tilde{\eta}_{0}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[k]

(6.40)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\pi_{1,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{2,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{3,+}^{(\alpha)}\pi_{4,+}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})

(6.41)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}2\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\pi_{1,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{2,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{3,-}^{(\beta)}\pi_{4,-}^{(\beta)}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\beta},\mathfrak{d}_{\beta},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

This time, the poles picked up from the D8-D8
′
part is the rank
n
PT4 counting with
(\vec{\pi}_{1,+},\vec{\pi}_{2,+},\vec{\pi}_{3,+},\vec{\pi}_{4,+})
, while the poles picked up from the
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
part is the DT4 counting with
(\vec{\pi}_{1,-},\vec{\pi}_{2,-},\vec{\pi}_{3,-},\vec{\pi}_{4,-})
. Strictly speaking the DT4 counting here is the one whose coordinates of boxes are reversed by
\epsilon_{a}\rightarrow-\epsilon_{a}
. We call this the (PT|DT) 4-vertex or the (PT|DT) partition function.

We also have the DT/PT correspondence for these vertices.

Theorem 6.6
.

The DT/PT correspondence of the (DT|PT) and (PT|DT) vertices is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\frac{y_{\beta}}{x_{\beta}}\right]\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\vec{\pi}_{1,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{2,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{3,\pm}\vec{\pi}_{4,\pm}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]

(6.42)

where

\displaystyle v_{\alpha}=e^{\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha}},\quad w_{\alpha}=e^{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}},\quad x_{\beta}=e^{\mathfrak{c}_{\beta}},\quad y_{\beta}=e^{\mathfrak{d}_{\beta}}.

(6.43)

For the surface boundary conditions, we similarly introduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I}),

(6.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})

\displaystyle=\left.\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0}_{\{\lambda_{A}\}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\phi_{I})\right|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\epsilon_{a}\rightarrow-\epsilon_{a}\\
\operatorname{sh}(x)\rightarrow-\operatorname{sh}(-x)\end{subarray}}.

Denoting the surface boundary conditions for the
n
pairs of the D8-D8
′
branes and
m
pairs of the
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
branes as
\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}
, the framing node contribution is

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\{\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{A,+}\}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\{\lambda^{(\beta)}_{A,-}\}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha},\phi_{I}).

(6.45)

We can define the (DT|PT) vertex

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[k]

(6.46)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\eta_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\{\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{A,+}\}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})

(6.47)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\{\lambda^{(\beta)}_{A,-}\}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

The poles picked up from the D8-D8
′
part is the DT4 configurations with surface boundary conditions
\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,+}\}
, while the poles picked up from the
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
part is the PT4 configurations with
\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,-}\}
.

Similarly, the (PT|DT) vertex is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{q}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[k]

(6.48)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{14,24,34})}{\operatorname{sh}(-\epsilon_{1,2,3,4})}\right)^{k}\oint_{\tilde{\eta}_{0}}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\frac{d\phi_{I}}{2\pi i}\prod_{I=1}^{k}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,+}_{\{\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{A,+}\}}(\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})

(6.49)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}8\text{--}\text{D}4\text{--}\text{D}0,-}_{\{\lambda^{(\beta)}_{A,-}\}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha},\phi_{I})\prod_{I<J}^{k}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{D}0\text{--}\text{D}0}(\phi_{I},\phi_{J}).

The poles picked up from the D8-D8
′
part gives the PT4 counting, while the
\overline{\text{D}8}\text{--}\overline{\text{D}8}^{\prime}
part gives the DT4 counting.

Theorem 6.7
.

The DT/PT correspondence of the (DT|PT) and (PT|DT) vertices is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{+}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]=\text{MF}\left[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{w_{\alpha}}{v_{\alpha}}\prod_{\beta=1}^{m}\frac{y_{\beta}}{x_{\beta}}\right]\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{\{\vec{\lambda}_{A,\pm}\}}[\mathfrak{q},q_{1,2,3,4}]

(6.50)

where

\displaystyle v_{\alpha}=e^{\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha}},\quad w_{\alpha}=e^{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha}},\quad x_{\beta}=e^{\mathfrak{c}_{\beta}},\quad y_{\beta}=e^{\mathfrak{d}_{\beta}}.

(6.51)

7
Conclusion and discussion

We gave a way to determine the framing node contribution and choose the contour to evaluate the partition function. We proposed that choosing the reference vector
\eta=\eta_{0}
gives the DT-side while choosing
\eta=\tilde{\eta}_{0}
gives the PT-side and have shown them explicitly for various examples for both the leg and surface boundary conditions. Although the framing node contribution we used was enough to perform practical computations, finding a combinatorial formula as the one given in
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
will be helpful for future use. How to determine the
J,E
-terms physically is also left for future work.

When we have only one-leg or two-legs, the poles appearing from the JK-residue formalism are always first order poles. For the three-legs case, second order poles appear similar to the PT3 case. For the four-legs case, third order poles appear and the computation is much more complicated. In this paper, we did not give a combinatorial way to understand and perform PT4 counting. Such kind of aspects need to be understood and is left for future work. The JK-residue formalism we showed might help the understanding. Moreover, we hope to produce computations for more examples elsewhere.

For the surface boundary conditions, new phenomena appeared. Depending on how the surfaces are placed, the PT4 vertex sometimes become trivial and sometimes become non-trivial but with only finite terms. The computations indeed reproduced results given in
Bae:2022pif
;
Bae:2024bpx
. Although in this paper, we only focused on cases when we have one, two, and three-surfaces only, generalizations to four, five, and six surfaces are straightforward. Giving a complete classification and formulas for such cases should not be tedious compared to the leg boundary conditions. Furthermore, one can combine the leg and surface boundary conditions generally using our formalism, though we omitted it to make the paper concise. A complete classification for such cases is also interesting. The PT vertex for surface boundary conditions coming from the JK-residue formalism here is the PT
0
vertex in
Bae:2022pif
;
Bae:2024bpx
. Understanding the derivation of PT
1
vertex dicussed there is also interesting.

We then discussed the DT/PT correspondence of the PT4 vertices. For low levels, they have been explicitly confirmed. Understanding the wall-crossing phenomenon from the supersymmetric quantum mechanics viewpoint is interesting. For the one-instanton level, it was obvious that the wall-crossing phenomenon occurred due to nontrivial residues at the asymptotics. Studying it for higher levels is also interesting. It was also shown recently in
Cao:2023lon
;
Piazzalunga:2023qik
;
Kimura:2024xpr
that the one-leg PT4 vertex appears as vortex partition functions for 3d
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theories appearing from D2-branes parallel to a D8-brane. The full PT4 vertex should appear from intersecting D2-branes inside a D8-brane. Studying the vortex partition functions and the wall-crossing of them might be also interesting. The PT4 vertex for surface boundary conditions are expected to appear from a setup with D4-branes inside a D8-brane. Understanding the corresponding partition function and the relation with the PT4 vertex is also interesting.

We also proposed generalizations of the DT4 and PT4 counting by additionally introducing anti-fundamental multiplets. Understanding the physical origin of these anti-fundamental multiplets is also interesting.

Although, we did not discuss the BPS/CFT correspondence of the PT4 partition functions, one can follow the strategy in
Kimura:2023bxy
;
Kimura:2024xpr
;
Kimura:2024osv
;
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
and study the free field realizations and the associated
qq
-characters. For the three-legs case, the second order pole similar gives derivatives of the operators as
Kimura-Noshita-PT3
. For the four-legs case, the situation is much more complicated and the third order pole introduce higher derivatives. This will be one of the new part for the 4-fold case. Another new part for the 4-fold case is the
qq
-character of the PT4 vertex with surface boundary conditions. Since the partition function terminates in finite terms, the same applies to the
qq
-characters. Understanding the algebraic interpretation of them might be interesting.

The authors thank Jiaqun Jiang, Satoshi Nawata, Jiahao Zheng for discussions on the JK-residue formalism.
The work of TK was supported by CNRS through MITI interdisciplinary programs, EIPHI Graduate School (No. ANR-17-EURE-0002) and Bourgogne-Franche-Comté region. GN is supported by JSPS KAKENHI Grant-in-Aid for JSPS fellows Grant No. JP25KJ0124.

Appendix A
JK-residue for degenerate poles

In this section, we briefly review the JK-residue formalism when we have degenerate poles
Jeffrey1993LocalizationFN
;
Szenes2003ToricRA
;
Brion1999ArrangementOH
(see also
Benini:2013xpa
;
Benini:2013nda
;
Hori:2014tda
;
Hwang:2014uwa
).

For a degenerate pole, we can associate a set of charge vectors as
Q_{\ast}=\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\}
with
n>k
. We then choose a sequence of
k
linearly independent charge vectors
Q_{j_{1}},\ldots Q_{j_{k}}
from
Q_{\ast}
and construct a flag
F
:

\displaystyle\{0\}\subset F_{1}\subset\cdots\subset F_{k}=\mathbb{R}^{k},\quad F_{i}=\text{span}\{Q_{j_{1}},\ldots,Q_{j_{k}}\}

(A.1)

The sequence
Q_{j_{1}},\ldots,Q_{j_{k}}
is called the basis
\mathcal{B}(F,Q_{\ast})
of the flag and it is not unique generally, but for a given flag we choose one of them. From the flag
F
and its basis
\mathcal{B}(F,Q_{\ast})
, a sequence of vectors is defined as

\displaystyle\kappa(F,Q_{\ast})=(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{k}),\quad\kappa_{i}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in Q_{\ast}\\
Q\in F_{a}\end{subarray}}Q.

(A.2)

The JK residue is then given as

\displaystyle\underset{\phi=\phi_{\ast}}{\text{JK-}\text{Res}}(Q(\phi_{\ast}),\eta)\mathcal{Z}(\phi)=\sum_{F}\delta(F,\eta)\frac{\text{sign}\det(\kappa(F,Q_{\ast}))}{\det\mathcal{B}(F,Q_{\ast})}\underset{\delta_{k}=0}{\Res}\cdots\underset{\delta_{1}=0}{\Res}\left.\mathcal{Z}(\phi)\right|_{Q_{i}(\phi)+f_{i}(\mathfrak{a},\epsilon_{a})=\delta_{a}}

(A.3)

where
\delta(F,\eta)
is
1
when the reference vector
\eta
is included in the closed cone spanned by
\kappa(F,Q_{\ast})
and
0
otherwise and the residue is performed in the order
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{k}
. Note also that the sum is taken over all possible flags. One can also confirm that the iterative residue given here reduces back to the definition for the non-degenerate poles discussed above.
